# A2. Project Executive Summary (max 300 words)

The Government of Rwanda (GGR), through its accredited entity MOE, is submitting a proposal to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to increase the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change in Gicumbi District in Northern Rwanda.

The project will be implemented over six years and will focus on four main outputs: improved land management and small-scale enterprises to support climate resilient practices; community support to implement sustainable forest management and adopt efficient cooking methods; successful adaptation and mitigation approaches communicated and mainstreamed at the national level. There are two additional outputs: improved water resources management and increased access to credit for adaptation.

The project will target the most vulnerable groups who have less resources to mitigate and adapt to climate change, including women-headed households, youth, people with disabilities, and those living in this category and women headed households who tend to be poor and are particularly vulnerable to climate change. The project will also focus on communities living close to or affected by land degradation, deforestation, landslides, flooding and droughts. A key focus will be on developing the adaptive capacity of farmers and local communities through improved access to credit for adaptation and the strengthening of local structures enabling them to continue adapting to future climate variability beyond the lifetime of the project.

The project comprises four interlinked outputs:

- Improved land management and small-scale enterprises to support climate resilient practices
- Community support to implement sustainable forest management and adopt efficient cooking methods
- Successful adaptation and mitigation approaches communicated and mainstreamed at the national level.
- There are two additional outputs: improved water resources management and increased access to credit for adaptation.

The project will make a significant contribution in taking forward the implementation of Rwanda's Green Growth and Climate Resilient Strategy (2011) as part of GGR’s commitment to addressing the risks of climate change. The project is aligned with the National Development Plan 2016-2035, the Vision 2040, and the strategy of the accredited entity MOE. FONERWA will manage the project on behalf of MOE.

The project has a high potential for replication in other vulnerable communities due to its low cost ratios for adaptation measures, and high cost-effectiveness for CO2 reductions for mitigation measures. The project is expected to benefit more than 380,000 people through mainstreaming interventions.

# B.1. Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Project

The Government of Rwanda is seeking GCF funding to support highly vulnerable communities in Northern Rwanda. The target area is characterized by pervasive poverty and livelihood insecurity due to the high dependence of the local population on rainfed, subsistence agriculture which is already

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 13

significantly affected by rising temperatures and unpredictable rainfall patterns. The majority of the project interventions target climate change risks and impacts, and are targeted towards vulnerable smallholder farmers, households, and communities in rural areas. As a Least Developed Country (LDC), Rwanda has limited access to concessional financing from multilateral development banks and there is limited potential to attract private sector finance. As an LDC, Rwanda's domestic financing is not sufficient to cover the project costs, which are estimated at USD 32,754,442. This amount represents approximately 10% of the country’s annual budget. While GCF can provide co-financing, funding support from GCF is critical to enable this project to be delivered.

MOE is requesting USD 32,754,442 from GCF as grant finance (excludes AE fee). Public sector grant- financed activities will be executed directly by FONERWA and implemented by GCF agencies either at District or Sector level. The proposed project components are designed to deliver the project outputs through a combination of investments that are not for profit adaptation, mitigation, capacity building and mainstreaming interventions to reduce vulnerability and enhance resilience in rural areas. These include: (i) climate-smart agriculture; (ii) forest management; (iii) climate resilient agriculture and the construction of low carbon social housing for highly vulnerable households.

A full financial and economic analysis has been undertaken for each of the components and an economic analysis is provided in Section F.1, and a financial analysis is provided in Section A.1 (Report No. 4).

Description on how the elements of financial instruments will concern concrete and action project outcomes, and leverage public and/or private finance.

The financial instruments available to MOE to use in delivering the project are limited due to its accreditation status with GCF. The only option for MOE is to use grant financing for small projects (above USD 10 million and up to USD 50 million with fiduciary functions) or Basic Fiduciary Functions (BFF). As a result, MOE will be unable to use the loan component as its only option as MINRENA's current accreditation status does not allow on-granting and lending. However, while the accreditation status of MINRENA is currently limited, it has been possible for MOE to access grants. For mitigation, grant elements have been tailored to address the incremental cost or the net premium required to implement the project component. For adaptation, grant elements have been designed to support the implementation of the project components and also to provide a conducive environment to address barriers, leveraging on public- and where possible - private finance.

See Economic Analysis (Report No 4) for details on how this will be applied to the proposal.

### C.1. Strategic Context

Despite being among Africa's poorest countries, Rwanda has achieved impressive growth and poverty reduction over the past decade. However, this progress has been fragile and unevenly distributed, with many communities still impoverished. The population, particularly women, are temporarily stalled in attracting private and external investment. However, Rwanda has made substantial progress in stabilizing and rehabilitating its economy.


---
**Page 9**
---

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 93

To pre-1994 levels. Although Rwanda is still dependent on aid with Official Development Assistance financing approximately 40% of the country's annual budget, Rwanda's economy has grown at 8% per year since 2001 with GDP increasing from $5 billion in 2000 to $36 billion in 2017. This is a remarkable achievement for such a small and landlocked country with the highest population density in Africa and a predominantly poor, rural population! highly dependent on agriculture. The country has made significant progress towards achieving its Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) targets and the OECD has considered Rwanda as one of the countries that uses aid most effectively.

Although the economy is growing and poverty levels are falling, Rwanda faces significant challenges in addressing climate change and adapting to a changing environment. Rwanda is the second most densely populated country in Africa with over 11 million people (half of whom is under 19) projected to rise to around 16 million by 2020, and 35% of its population lives below the poverty line.

Much of Rwanda's economy depends directly upon land, water and biodiversity resources. The majority of the country's population live in rural areas where they are highly dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods and health, especially marginalised groups such as women who often do not have equal access to other sources of income. Most of the country’s electricity is generated from hydropower with a small amount being generated from (small-scale) hydropea. There is a very high dependence on biomass for fuel and rain-fed agriculture is still the mainstay of the economy.

The majority of Rwanda's population live in areas where more than 70% of the cultivated land surface has slopes greater than 10%, which results in high levels of erosion and sedimentation, particularly during heavy rainfall. This makes Rwanda highly vulnerable to climate change. These are the underlying factors which make Rwanda’s rural population so vulnerable to climate change.

## Climate change context

Current meteorological data and early climate change lead to major impacts in Rwanda. Rwanda experiences high levels of climatic variability and natural hazards due to the influence of El Niño - Southern Oscillation (ENSO) events, which are associated with increased rainfall during the wet season and droughts during the dry season. Historic droughts in the East African floods occurred in 2007, 2005, 2006, 2008, 2009, 2013, and 2016 and these have had a significant impact on food security and water availability. The country has experienced an increase of 1% in GDP. Rwanda also experiences periodic droughts, with major events in 1998-2000, 2005-2006 and 2010-2012. These have had a significant impact on food security and water availability. The country has experienced an increase of 1% in GDP.

The future economic cost of climate change in Rwanda will be large – estimated at an additional impact of 3% of GDP each year by 2050. Analysis of the global temperature rise across range of future representative concentration pathways (RCPs) shows that temperatures are likely to increase in Rwanda and rainfall hot days. The changes in rainfall and land use intensity are more uncertain, but an increase in heavy rainfall is expected for Rwanda. The analysis also suggests that rainfall will become less reliable and these changes are likely to exacerbate the impacts of current climate variability in Rwanda, as well as lead to new risks.

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 93


### Rwanda's sensitivity to climate change was assessed as high and its adaptive capacity low in a national study on climate vulnerability.

The rising rural population is increasing demands for settlement expansion and the provision of infrastructure, which are leading to deforestation. This, together with other human activities such as livestock grazing, hunting, and fishing, is causing stressings including poverty, land degradation, food insecurity, rising epidemics and natural disasters. Expected impacts include increased vulnerability to climate change, reduced agricultural productivity, and loss of livelihoods.

### A baseline survey on household vulnerability to climate change found high levels of climate vulnerability throughout the country.

The study identified factors such as low adaptive capacity, poor infrastructure, lack of access to information, poor land tenure security, poor soil fertility, poor water resources management, poor livestock management practices, poor agricultural extension services, and poor health care facilities. The study also highlighted that the majority of households are dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods.

### Climate factors, exacerbated by the loss of forest and vegetation cover, steep slopes and high dependence on traditional rain-fed agriculture, are causing a variety of impacts.

The high number of poor people engaged in subsistence farming is leading to overexploitation of natural resources. The study also found that agricultural production has been decreasing over time at the expense of pasture, natural forests and fallows. Over exploitation of land and water resources has led to soil erosion, reduced fertility, and increased sedimentation.

### Environmental degradation and climate change have been recognized as the highest political level, as well as a major challenge for development in Rwanda.

The government has developed several strategies and action plans that aim at addressing these challenges. However, there is still need to develop adequate responses to climate change which is consistent throughout national policies and strategies. The National Strategy for Transformation (NST) 2017-2035, the Vision 2040, and the National Action Plan on Development in Rwanda 2018-2022 all recognize that environmental degradation and climate change are major challenges to development.

However, there is still need to develop adequate responses to climate change which is consistent throughout national policies and strategies. The National Strategy for Transformation (NST) 2017-2035, the Vision 2040, and the National Action Plan on Development in Rwanda 2018-2022 all recognize that environmental degradation and climate change are major challenges to development.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 10 OF 93

### Budget Call Category (BCC). However, limited capacity to tackle climate change issues particularly in productive sectors such as agriculture reduces national capacity to adopt and implement the GCMGRS.

Rwanda's Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) includes a number of actions that will be supported by the Green Climate Fund. These include: 1) Promoting sustainable land management practices, including kitchen gardens, nutrient recycling, and water conservation; organic waste composting; maintaining sustainable pest control systems; and promoting agroforestry; 2) Promoting climate-smart agriculture through increasing irrigation and water management including summer harvesting; afforestation through enhanced perennial vegetation; and the use of improved seeds and varieties; 3) Promoting the sustainable use of degraded forest resources; and sustainable use of biomass fuels through the increased uptake of improved cookstoves.

Rwanda's National Adaptation Plan of Action (NAPA) articulates Rwanda’s strategy to reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts. The NAPA identifies six priority areas: 1) Water resource management, including landfills, drought and low flows, extreme temperatures and heat waves; the six NAPA priorities are: 2) Forests and land use; 3) Agriculture and food security; 4) Energy and water supply; 5) Health and sanitation; meteorological system and rapid intervention mechanisms; 3) Promotion of non-agricultural income generating activities, including livestock production and diversification of species resistant to environmental changes; and 6) Development of firewood alternative sources of energy.

Rwanda has identified Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMA) across seven priority sectors: agriculture, water resources management, land use, health and sanitation, energy and water supply, forests and land use, increasing the basic productivity of Rwanda’s forests is a priority. As land for afforestation is a very scarce resource in Rwanda, the NAMA proposes to plant 10 million trees on degraded lands and abandoned agricultural areas; new plantations to achieve a national tree cover forest of about 30%, with particular attention to steep slopes in order to combat soil erosion and improve water retention. The NAMA also proposes to establish a National Forest Service to promote the historical importance of the sector and the economic potential for value addition, job creation and the potential for carbon sequestration.

The Sector Strategy Paper (SSP) for the Forestry and Land Use Sector highlights the use of efficient cookstoves, LED lighting and solar water heating along with Off Grid Solar Power. Rwanda has closed 80% of its gender gap—outspending many countries in the Global North, including the United Kingdom, France, Germany, Italy, Spain, and Japan. Rwanda is ranked as one of the top 10 countries on the “Gender Index,” with the next sub-Saharan African country, Burundi, ranking 12th. Since 2008, Rwanda has had a female president, Pauline Kagame, who was elected in 2007 and re-elected in 2015. The gender gap in primary education has closed by 64% representation. In recent years, the GCF has made concerted efforts to connect gender equality and climate change adaptation and mitigation. Rwanda’s NAMA includes a number of actions that will be supported by the Fund, including measures, from identifying gender as a cross-cutting issue to including specific goals and targets for women's empowerment in all sectors. The NAMA also proposes to strengthen the capacity of women to participate fully in their country's development process, recognizing women’s important roles in sustainable development and climate change adaptation, mitigation and resilience building.

Building on its experience with the GCF, Rwanda has developed a gender action plan that is supported by a wide network of stakeholders in civil society and the private sector that consider gender a cross-cutting issue and an essential element for achieving the objectives of the Fund. The GCF Gender Action Plan includes specific strategies, institutions, women's groups, and more with details on the inclusion of gender or lack, can be found in the GCF Gender Action Plan. Rwanda has also established a National Gender Focal Point (NGFP) to identify policies and strategies relevant to each component.

Rwanda has developed a comprehensive institutional framework, and has established agencies to work cross-sectorally to support natural resource management; notably REMA and the Rwanda Water and Forests Authority (RWFA). The Rwanda Environment Management Authority (REMA) was established in 2013 under Climate Change (FONERWA) has been established to address sector cross- sector financing needs. Rwanda also recognizes the importance of gender equality, and has established a National Gender Focal Point (NGFP) under cross-sectoral planning meetings and the Joint Action Development Forums (JADF), consultative platforms used for promoting cooperation between the private sector, civil society and the public sector.

### C2. Project Objective at Baseline

The project will contribute to Rwanda’s NAMA by supporting the implementation of a gender-sensitive approach in the development and implementation of climate change adaptation policies and strategies.

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 11 OF 93

The proposed project will increase the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change in Gicumbi District in Northern Rwanda. This will be achieved by restoring and enhancing ecosystem services of one of the most important river basins in the country, the Musambara River watershed. The project will also aim at improving livelihoods through sustainable land management practices that are adapted to climate change. It will support community-based adaptation planning and livelihoods diversification. Knowledge and capacity developed under this project will be shared with other communities in the region.

The Musambara River is a trans-boundary river, flowing from Uganda into Rwanda. The basin covers nine sectors in Gicumbi—Kangra, Rubaya, Cyumba, Rukurubu, Shangasira, Mukungwa, Managasiyo, Byimba2—and Bugesera Districts. It drains an area of 105 km² and has a catchment elevation ranging from 984 to 3,670 metres above sea level. A map of the target area is shown in Annex 4 and detailed maps of the watershed and sub-watersheds are provided in Annexes 2 and 3.

The project will specifically target the most vulnerable groups who have less resources to mitigate and adapt to climate change. This includes the extreme poor, as more than a quarter of households in the target area fall below the poverty line (10% for rural areas). The project will also focus on women and youth, who are particularly vulnerable to climate change. In particular, many of the project's interventions target those who farm on marginal land and are very highly dependent on rainfed agriculture.

The project will work with communities, local government authorities, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and local institutions to ensure that the developed resilience is embedded within communities enabling them to cope better with climate change. The project will also support the development of a community-based adaptation plan for each of the nine sectors in Gicumbi District.

Investments are described for the three areas of intervention: 1) Watershed protection and climate resilient agriculture; 2) Climate-resilient livelihoods diversification; and 3) Community-based adaptation planning and management.

**Result:** The degradation of the Musambara watershed is being accelerated by climate change and local communities have low adaptive capacity.


## Projected Water Demand

The Musambara River originates in the Mubarema and Nyagwamba river catchments and 60% of its land area is situated in the degraded Musumba watershed. Gicumbi extends north from Kigali to the Uganda border and has a population density of about 1,250 people per km² (378 people per km² for rural areas). The majority live on small plots with 91.7ha in rural areas). The Munyumba River is trans-boundary with Uganda and located in the Bubanza District.

The basin is on the boundary of the Albertine Rift Biodiversity Hot Spot which is one of the most important regions for biodiversity conservation in Africa, particularly for primates and birds. It also has a high proportion of forested land in mainland Africa. In particular, a significant portion of wetlands in the Munyumba (which play an important role in food production) are located on the Uganda border.

The Musambara River is one of the most important rivers in Rwanda for smallholder farming and agricultural use. As small holder farming predominates, the local population is highly dependent on the ecosystem services provided by the river basin. The project area has a high rainfall intensity (100-250 mm per day) during the dry season.

The Musambara River is characterized by steep topography and shallow soils with limited integration of trees and shrubs within the landscape, with intense rainfall leading to high soil erosion and floods and landslides. The project area experiences soil erosion rates between 130-500 tonnes/hectare/year. This leads to major economic damage, with an estimated cost of US$24 per hectare per year (US$8 per ha for land degradation and US$16 per ha for sediment load). The projected variability in rainfall combined with high levels of erosion and intense rainfall will lead to a further increase in soil loss rates. This is expected to be between 300-500 tonnes/hectare/year up to 5.2 million USD per year. The projected variability in rainfall combined with high levels of erosion and intense rainfall will lead to a further increase in soil loss rates. This is expected to be between 300-500 tonnes/hectare/year up to 5.2 million USD per year.

Projected water demand up to 2040 shows that water requirements for all sectors will increase by 83% of the current levels, with a projected increase in demand from agriculture and livestock farming of about 170% during the dry season.

(10% of the land comprises slopes between 25-55%) make the area prone to landslides that could cause the destruction of physical infrastructure downstream and downstream.

The ongoing degradation of natural resources is most evident in rural communities. Agricultural yields are very low, with a high degree of variability due to climate change and other factors such as soil erosion and nutrient depletion. Poor land management practices have led to the loss of topsoil and reduced fertility. The limited use of inputs and mechanization, erosion on steep slopes, lack of irrigation and post-harvest losses are also major constraints.

The high levels of poverty limit the adaptive capacity of communities to invest in climate-resilient agricultural systems. Small-scale farmers are particularly vulnerable to climate variability. Agriculture is the main occupation for 84% of the district's population. The main crops are tea (Gicumbi hosts one of the largest tea plantations in Rwanda) and coffee, which are grown on small plots of land. The average size of land holding per household is approximately 0.7 ha and productivity levels are very low due to soil fertility depletion, limited use of inputs and mechanization, erosion on steep slopes, a lack of irrigation and post-harvest losses.

### USAID - $1 million per year, directly reducing the incomes of smallholders.

#### Climate change is affecting coffee production, both in relation to direct production but also from the increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (e.g., droughts, floods, and heatwaves). This has a negative impact on yields (by value) and supports the livelihoods of several hundred thousand smallholder farmers across the country (30% of coffee producers are women).

#### Smallholders in Rwanda are particularly vulnerable to climate change. They have limited resources and their livelihoods are at risk from climate change, with major parts of current production areas (~50%) expected to be affected by 2050.

### Future climate projections indicate critical risks to smallholders involved in tea and coffee production. In the early stages, early climate change will definitely affect the productivity and quality of tea and coffee in Rwanda. In the medium term (by 2030), climate change will have a significant impact on the livelihoods of smallholder farmers.

#### The global area suitable for coffee production is by as much as 50% by 2050** and analyses in Asia has shown that the areas most at risk are those with high rainfall variability. Smallholders in Rwanda are particularly important because they are long lived crops, much more so than cereals, and thus land use under cultivation can be maintained for a longer period of time.

#### This issue is important because Rwanda is currently expanding the area under tea and coffee cultivation, as part of its strategy to diversify its economy. The expansion of these crops will increase their vulnerability to climate change in the future. Climate change will also lead to additional impacts from pests and disease vectors which are already present on the tea and coffee farms.

#### Smallholders in Rwanda are particularly vulnerable because they have limited resources, such as capital, labor, which are committed to temperature (with higher levels in warmer areas). Future climate change will affect productivity and quality of tea and coffee. In the early stages, early climate change will definitely affect the productivity and quality of tea and coffee; the window of opportunity for making the tea and coffee sectors resilient is urgent.

#### The WFP project aims at improving the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers in Rwanda to climate change impacts. This project operates in different districts (Nyamagabe, Gasabo, Kigali Rural, Gicumbi) and will be replicated in other districts. The project's main objective is to improve the adaptive capacity of natural systems and livelihoods of smallholders through the implementation of climate-smart interventions.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 14 OF 33


The Gulu West Water and Land Management Project
2010-2013
MURUGU and RWTA USD 5.7 million over three years (funded by GCF)

In the degraded landscape of Gulu in Western Uganda's province, the GWLM project worked closely with communities to restore degraded land, reduce soil erosion, rehabilitate hillsides and optimise land use. This mountainous area is characterised by steep slopes, shallow soils, landslides and flooding. The GWLM work undertaken detailed six avenues for intervention: slope stabilisation; land husbandry interventions; Steep hillside were protected with perennial grasses and shrubs planted on contour lines. Soil erosion was reduced through the construction of terraces constructed on slopes 0-15% with soil depths between 50- 100cm, and by planting trees and shrubs to reduce soil erosion. These measures were combined with biological measures such as intercropping, crop rotation and tree planting: For slopes ranging from 16-40% and a soil depth of greater than 30 cm, the project planted grasses (Kikuyu grass, Phalaris aquatic etc), forage legumes and trees (Acacia tortilis, Acacia mellifera, Erythrina abyssinica) to stabilise slopes. For slopes with less than 16% and commercial tree growth is high in these areas were replanted with indigenous species such as Acacia tortilis and Erythrina abyssinica.

Note: The Mwamurwa catchment plan also proposes a food security system in Mabira in Gulu District to increase water yield, reduce soil erosion, improve land productivity and reduce the risk of landslides and flooding and to enhance the resilience of small holder tea and coffee producers to climate change. This project will be implemented by the Ministry of Water and Environment (MoWE) and funded by the World Bank.

Expected outcomes and impact that the project will aim to achieve in improving baseline scenario 1

* **Water restored and agricultural practices resilient to climate change**
    * The project has improved water yield, reduced soil erosion, increased land productivity and reduced the risk of landslides and flooding and to enhance the resilience of small holder tea and coffee producers to climate change.
    * The project will also improve the stability of hillsides and increase carbon sequestration with 17.859 ha of land or forest under improved management practices, which will contribute towards reducing greenhouse gas emissions and help build climate resistance by ensuring optimal flow rates, reducing sedimentation and related costs to downstream water users.
    * The project will also reduce the risk of landslides and flooding in the catchment area through terraces and landslide barriers. These essential ecosystem services are needed to support sustainable, resilient livelihoods.

* **Increasing vegetative cover and enhancing soil conservation will reduce erosion, increase soil fertility and overall productivity**
    * The project has increased vegetative cover on 105 ha of land and enhanced soil conservation on 234 ha of land. Farmers will be supported to implement agro-ecological approaches to increase climate resilience. These varieties include drought tolerant crops such as sorghum, pearl millet, pigeon pea, cowpea, groundnut and sweet potato. The project has also provided training in water harvesting techniques including to rural poverty reduction. Farmer promoters and government extension workers will also be supported to disseminate information on the benefits of agro-ecological practices.
    * The project will reach a total of 35,000 farmers by the end of the project. Support to small scale tea and coffee producers to adopt climate resilient agricultural practices is expected to increase their productivity and income. This in turn exposes the slopes to tillage and soil erosion. In all, current resilience climate will be rolled out over 4225 ha of land. The project will also provide training on water harvesting techniques including rainwater harvesting for small holder farmers. Adaptation planning with communities and investment in a community adaptation fund will be provided to support the development of local action plans to address climate change risks for vulnerable lands and establishing protective forests. This will strengthen the linkage between watershed protection, land use management and poverty reduction and will lead to target vulnerable populations.

Baselines: Productive forest resources are degraded and poorly managed

Forest productivity is around 25 – 33% of potential because silviculture practice is almost universally sub-optimal and affected by variable rainfall and low quality seed and wood regeneration. The project will improve the use of wood regeneration techniques, including planting and natural regeneration. Forests also support a series of ecosystem services such as water yield, soil conservation, biodiversity, carbon sequestration and timber production as well as biodiversity protection, groundwater and stream regulation, flood control and regulators of regional and global climate.

Community grants to the GWLM will be provided by FOREKNA (no GCF finance will be used for the grants. GCF finance will only be used to provide the TA to support the set up and operation of the fund).

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 15 OF 93


| **Microclimates:** Forest cover in the target area is characterised by small, discrete, scattered clusters of varying size across the hilly landscape. Shallow soils predominate, the available phosphorus levels are very low and soil acidity is high (pH <4). The average rainfall is 1200 mm per year with a relatively even distribution throughout the year (average rainfall?). The predominance of genetically degraded Eucalyptus species which are not well suited to the local climate, poor land preparation and establishment practices, and lack of post-establishment care have resulted in a range of forest goods and services available for livelihoods, household and economic opportunities, increasing vulnerability to fire and wind damage. This has led to widespread degradation of forests, particularly on steep slopes under poor site conditions, poor site preparation and establishment practice, late planting and little or no tending. This combined with the lack of a sustainable management plan and the absence of any form of forest certification, coupled with too densely spaced young plantations provides a reservoir for pest build up and attack. Adoption of improved practices is essential to reverse this trend.


| **Coppicing and harvesting is undertaken with axes and pangas, damaging stools and compromising regrowth:** Very few people (mostly men) are employed in wood processing and there is a lack of drying and storage facilities. The majority of the timber is sold as firewood or for charcoal production, generating large volumes of wood wastage. Moreover, harvesting, especially within small private woodlots, is undertaken early, often after only 3-5 years from planting. This results in poor quality timber with low value. In addition, the conversion of forest resources from forest resources to Guncate inevitably erodes the household asset base needed to cope with climate change.


| **Poverty and lack of access to alternative livelihoods:** The majority of households are engaged in activities being cut over (legally and illegally) resulting in a sparsely covered degraded forest estate. ## Expected outcomes and impact that the project will aim to achieve in improving baseline scenario 2

### Increased productivity of forest resources and reduced deforestation

- The project will assist forest owners and users to improve forest productivity and timber quality to support global market demand for wood products, while reducing pressure on forests through improved harvesting of wood for household cooking and Mulindi tea factory to reduce pressure on forest and wood resources.
- The project will increase the uptake of sustainable forest management practices on woodlots, forests and plantations to increase the volume of timber sustainably available for meeting fuelwood and other timber needs. Improved planning and implementation of forest management activities through a series of trial plots will demonstrate best practice forest management, improved forest planning, seed quality and availability, and better harvesting techniques.
- The project will reduce waste during harvesting and processing. Benefits will accrue not only in terms of products harvested but also to the environment by reducing deforestation, increasing carbon sequestration and biodiversity values emerging from the presence of forests. Proposed forestry activities include: 1) establishment of 200 ha of new forest plantations (including 45 ha of Acacia senegal (baobam) with 37 new cooperatives active in managing nurseries and beekeeping).
- Forests will be managed to reduce the risk of fire, which is a major threat to forests. The project has a positive impact on climate change mitigation with 140,046 CO2eq/ha in reduced emissions over the project lifetime. This is achieved by increasing the number of forests and conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks over the lifetime of the project.
- The project will also reduce the pressure on Gicumbi's forests from reducing biomass use in energy production and improving fuelwood harvesting techniques. The project will establish 100 ha of new plantations, an additional 900 hectares and 40 large public buildings using biogas, and wood chip producing facilities. This is expected to reduce the amount of firewood used by households from 25 kg/day to 3 kg/day reducing greenhouse gas emissions of 59,261 CO2eq/ha over the lifetime of the project.

### Based on a Rapid Assessment of Forest Resources in Gicumbi District and findings limited access to water and other essential services

The year 1994 saw the displacement of more than 3 million people from neighbouring countries to Rwanda following the 1994 genocide created an immediate impact on natural resources. While most of the 2 population were able to return home, many others did not. The government has since identified over 50,000 returnees who had fled during the earlier 1994 massacres were resettled on public and state owned land in Gicumbi District.

The scarcity of land, population growth and the increasing intensity of extreme weather events mean that significant numbers of people live in areas at risk from flooding and landslides. Many areas of Rwanda are prone to flooding and landslides due to heavy rainfall and poor drainage systems. In 2015, 38% of the country's population is exposed to landslide risk and an estimated 23% of Gicumbi's land area has moderate or high vulnerability to landslides.

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 17 OF 93

**Who are affected by landslides and floods including 14,254 in Giumbi district (one of the highest in the country). Households living on slopes are affected by landslides because people have been practicing intensive farming with little or no soil conservation measures. In some cases, they have built houses on unstable land without any thing to prevent soil erosion. Households living in valleys or near takes and rivers are also at risk from flooding which is exacerbated by the lack of proper drainage systems.**

The risks of landslides and floods are exacerbated by poorly constructed housing made using weak materials. Around 65% of vulnerable households in the country constructed with sun-dried bricks while 29% used wood and mud as construction material. The remaining 16% used other materials such as stones, bamboo, etc.

The challenges for inhabitants of Giumbi district include an inadequate water supply and poor sanitation, poor waste management, lack of access to health services, and limited access to education. Most households in Giumbi district have access to electricity.

Many households are also considered vulnerable because they are unable to withstand climatic shocks. The worst affected are the extreme poor households** that are landless and have no livestock or other assets to fall back on during a disaster. These households often live in areas prone to landslides, floods, and droughts where they lack access to their neighbours or the community government to provide labour and materials to rebuild homes if destroyed. Others who are considered vulnerable include women-headed households, people with disabilities, children under five years old but struggle to obtain the finances to rebuild homes, and rely on the government to provide roofing and housing materials. The elderly also face challenges in rebuilding their homes because they often lack the financial resources or are self-sufficient** cannot withstand continued climatic shocks as these will deplete the asset base. Women-headed households are considered highly vulnerable because they have limited access to credit and savings, and are also considered highly vulnerable as they are often from poorer segments of Rwandan society; therefore have limited access to land, water, and other resources that are critical for their livelihoods. Children under five years old at risk as they more easily get sick or die due to the instability of the home (landslides or flowing water or flood) and lack of access to health services.

The Gulf has a programme to support help vulnerable and poor families to relocate to safe areas where the Gulf provides access to services and utilities (schools, water, electricity etc.). The Rwanda Housing Authority’s (RHA) flagship project is the Model Village Project which aims at providing housing for those most affected by slope instability, soil erosion or other factors, many of which are either directly caused by, or indirectly impacted by climate change. The Gulf has provided funding to RHA to construct 100 model villages and build new houses in rural settlements on safe ground which are then donated to the poorest and most vulnerable families who have been displaced due to landslides, floods, droughts, etc.

Currently, each province has a such a village with access to water, electricity, a market, a school, co-operatives and playgrounds. The Gulf is planning to construct two Model Village projects in Giumbi district as part of the phase 2 of the programme.

### Consequences of initial cereal events results in flood and landslides

The seasonality of rainfall events results in flood and landslides especially during the rainy season. Many settlements are located on top of hills and have inadequate drainage and water management, culminating in flooding and landslides. The main causes of these floods include heavy rains, poor soil structure, run-off of storm water and causing soil erosion. Run-off following intense precipitation is exacerbated by rapid discharge from runoff channels which results in the formation of debris flows and mudslides. These are common during the rainy season in the valley. A number of major gulches have opened up especially around Byumba.

Conversely, many areas experience drought during the dry season. The problem is more pronounced along the districts with high rainfall intensity and with quite a diverse topography. Many inhabitants have no access to water supply or sanitation services and only 40% of the population has access to piped water. Access to safe water varies between 40 – 50%. Women and children are most affected by this situation as they spend up to four hours per day collecting water, while men spend less than an hour a day on this task. People in rural areas spend more time per day to complete this task, are especially impacted by water shortages as this significantly increases their work burden. The lack of access to safe drinking water also results in the spread of water-borne diseases and other tasks. Water demand from the Munyabwa watershed is projected to almost quadruple by 2032 and water use in 250 households will be increased by more than five times the demand in 2015. By 2029 it is expected to be shortage of water for six months of the year** This calls for collective action to improve water storage and management to avert shortages in the future.

## Expected outcomes and impact that the project will aim to achieve in improving baseline scenario 3:
### Reduced exposure of human settlements to flooding and landslides
The project will reduce flood risk by reducing the accumulation of water by reducing run-off and increasing infiltration on ridges and the upper slope and increase water storage capacity to reduce the volume of water flowing into rivers. The project will also reduce landslide risk through improved drainage, lowland flooding, siltation and soil damage as well as reduce sediment loads in water courses.
This investment will be made by constructing 100 new houses in Kasese and 200 new houses installed with rainwater harvesting and storage systems and run-off control measures (vegetation, diversion ditches, check dams) in Kabale.

This will improve decision-making and project interventions to increase capacity to assess, manage and monitor risks and will result in more comprehensive adaptation planning at the national level. In the agricultural sector, it is expected that this will lead to a shift from a traditional risk management approach to develop a range of adaptation options; will help to expand and transform production using a climate resilient approach; and start early planning for long-term climate change.

1. The upfront investment costs needed to shift Rwanda toward more intense resilient green growth are a significant barrier for pursuing these development pathways. The total additional capital costs of shifting to Intensive Resilient Green Growth (IRGG) in the agricultural sector is estimated at USD 20 billion, or an increase of USD 2.7 bn (cumulative by 2030) compared to business as usual across three key sectors (water, agriculture, energy). This includes costs for land preparation and infrastructure development, including irrigation systems and pursuit green development pathways. additional sources of finance will be needed that can bear these larger financial requirements taking into account additional technical risks associated with the High Green growth scenario.

2. The high population density and the country's reliance on agriculture for livelihoods is a key barrier to the implementation of climate change adaptation strategies in Rwanda. This results in increased pressure on natural resources as a last order when a significant part of the population largely lacks other assets (land, education, etc.). In addition, there are also limited opportunities for diversification and income generation activities. The lack of access to water is another major barrier to the implementation of climate change adaptation strategies in Rwanda. This results in increased pressure on natural resources as a last order when a significant part of the population largely lacks other assets (land, education, etc.). In addition, there are also limited opportunities for diversification and income generation activities.

3. At the same time, pervasive poverty means that communities don't have the means to invest in long term adaptation strategies. This results in increased pressure on natural resources as a last order when a significant part of the population largely lacks other assets (land, education, etc.). In addition, there are also limited opportunities for diversification and income generation activities.

4. Lack of technical know-how that cuts across the proposed areas of intervention including: sustainable forest management practices, agroforestry systems, soil conservation techniques, improved livestock breeds, carbon building in and reducing the carbon footprint of cookstoves and bio-factories. These skills are either absent or not well developed in Rwanda. This results in increased pressure on natural resources as a last order when a significant part of the population largely lacks other assets (land, education, etc.). In addition, there are also limited opportunities for diversification and income generation activities.

5. Traditional gender roles and patriarchal attitudes towards women in rural Rwanda means that women have very little say over land use decisions or access to resources such as water and livestock. This results in increased pressure on natural resources as a last order when a significant part of the population largely lacks other assets (land, education, etc.). In addition, there are also limited opportunities for diversification and income generation activities.

Describe the objectives and the general interest of the project as being in each of its components.

Provide information on how the activities are linked to objectives, outputs and outcomes that the project intends to achieve. The objectives, outputs, and outcomes for this project are described below:

### C3.1 Summary of proposed interventions

The project will work with vulnerable communities to climate change in Gicumbi District in Northern Rwanda. The project will restore and enhance ecosystem services in a major sub-catchment of the Musanze River Basin. It will also provide technical assistance, training and capacity building for smallholders and support smallholders to adopt climate resilient agriculture. The project will also invest in green settlements for improved livelihoods diversification.

The project interventions are designed to increase climate resilience by:

- **Improved management of land and forest resources**
- **Sustainable land management and sustainable grazing**
- **Enhanced agroforestry**
- **Increased food security**

### Project Interventions to increase climate resilience

| Intervention | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Organization of the farmers' association | Strengthened adaptive capacity through improved organization, leadership and governance. |
| Sustainable land management and sustainable grazing | Sustainable land management practices that improve soil fertility and water retention in the catchment area. |
| Enhanced agroforestry | Improved livelihoods diversification by increasing income from non-farm activities such as beekeeping, apiculture, and other economic activities. |
| Increased food security | Increased food security through improved access to markets for agricultural products. |

### Drivers of climate vulnerability

- Lack of knowledge on climate change
- Limited capacity in climate change adaptation planning and livelihood diversification.
- Poor land use practices and lack of sustainable land management.

## Figure 2: Theory of change

The project will focus on one of Rwanda’s most climate vulnerable districts, Gicumbi. Interventions will particularly target poor, climate vulnerable households located in nine sectors of Gicumbi district (Rutabu, Cyangura, Kanyiga, Muhanga, Nyamagabe, Nyanza, Rukara, Rubavu and Rugenda). These areas are characterized by high poverty levels (40% of the district’s population) live. There are three underlying principles to the project: 1) supporting climate resilient and sustainable livelihoods; 2) building capacity for adaptation at all levels including households, communities, districts and beyond, transferable to other areas of Rwanda; and 3) increasing knowledge and understanding of climate risk among key decision makers.

The project comprises four interlinked components: (1) watershed protection and climate resilient agriculture; (2) sustainable land management and soil health improvement; (3) building capacity for adaptation at all levels including climate resilient livelihoods, communities, districts and beyond; and 4) Knowledge transfer and mainstreaming. The corresponding project outputs are:

* **Output 1:** Sub-catchments of the Mwamba watershed restored and small-scale enterprises and coffee farmers supported to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices (A7.2)
* **Output 2:** Sustainable land management and soil health improved in the Mwamba watershed (A7.3)
* **Human settlements developed and/or modified to increase climate resilience; and**
* **Output 4:** Capacity for adaptation at all levels including households, communities, districts and beyond built at the national level.

There are two expected outcomes from the project, linked to both mitigation and adaption: improved management of water resources (A7.1); and increased greenhouse gas emissions reductions (M3.2), and 2) strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks (A7.5).

### C.3.1.1 Main activities under each component

#### Component 1: Watershed protection and climate resilient agriculture (USD 12.6 million)

The component will improve the stability of the watershed to support climate resilient livelihoods and reduce the vulnerability of households in the Mwamba catchment area, while also reducing greenhouse gas emissions from the effects of flooding, erosion, over-cultivation and human encroachment. This component will support the implementation of a range of activities that will contribute to improved water quality and quantity, increased climate resilience by promoting appropriate land use and agricultural practices to minimize erosion and restore ecosystem services in the watershed catchment area.

* **1:** Strengthen community-based adaptation
* **2:** Improve soil health and increase crop yields through agroforestry
* **3:** Stationary trees, reforesting and steep slopes with protective terraces
* **4:** Planting of drought-resistant crops (e.g. sorghum)
* **5:** Support 1000 wormhole crop-livestock farmers to adopt eco-ecological approaches to increase climate-resilient livelihoods in the Mwamba watershed catchment area (A7.2, A7.3, A7.4, A7.5, M3.2)
* **6:** Support 1000 wormhole crop-livestock farmers to adopt eco-ecological approaches to increase climate-resilient livelihoods in the Mwamba watershed catchment area (A7.2, A7.3, A7.4, A7.5, M3.2)
* **7:** Integrate climate resilient practices into local extension services
* **8:** Provide training and technical assistance to farmers on climate-resilient agricultural practices to increase productivity and reduce losses from weather climate change
* **9:** Increase capacity for pest monitoring, surveillance and PMI at area current and future climate change
* **10:** Support 5000 households in the Mwamba watershed catchment area to plant trees and protect slopes in the watershed (planning for future climate change)

#### Soil and water management

Soil and water management will be promoted to reduce climate vulnerability, improve soil and water quality and increase the range and quality of the goods and services available for livelihood and economic use. This component will support the implementation of a range of activities that will contribute to improved water quality and quantity in the Mwamba watershed catchment area.

As well as enhancing adaptation to current climate variability and future climate change, the intervention will also improve soil health and soil improvements as well as provide significant livelihood, poverty alleviation and biodiversity benefits. The component will be implemented by a team of staff recruited to the project and based in Gicumbi District Headquarter Office (DHO) with technical support from the relevant district government and research agencies and institutions such as:

* **National Agricultural Research Institute (NARI):** Soil health improvement activities (1.1-6.4)
* **National Agriculture Export Development Board (NAEB):** For tea and coffee interventions (1.6-1.9)
* **National Environment Management Authority (NEMA):** Water quality improvement interventions (1.1.3, 8)
* **Rwanda METEO:** for climate and weather services (1.7)

The implementation arrangements are explained in detail in Section C.7.

### Sub-component 5.1: Strengthening community-based adaptation (USD 60 million)

This sub-component will increase the understanding by local stakeholders of the impacts of climate change and the value of conserving and protecting steep slopes and riparian buffer zones as well as the benefits of integrated land use management, water resource management and soil conservation interventions. Empowering farmers through participatory engagement to make informed decisions will ensure real ownership of the projects and their outcomes. The project will support communities in engaging with communities through existing cooperatives and community groups and build the capacities of rural communities to adapt to climate change by providing them with information on climate change, sharing local knowledge into community planning processes through linking communities to experts. The approach will be to raise farmers' awareness about climate change impacts and how they can adapt to these changes in order to understand the climate threats which affect them and support them in taking appropriate steps to plan for and enhance their resilience against future climate shocks. This will also help communities to better assess their capacity to assess their own needs. The evolving nature of adaptation in future climate trends are uncertain, meaning that it is important to provide farmers with information on how they can adapt to these changes by learning and rectifying possible mal-adaptation and exchanging the information. The Government's role is to create the right conditions for this.

The project will support local people to identify specific areas for investment in improvements that promote integrated land and water management. The participation of local communities in these initial assessments will foster ownership, commitment and a sense of responsibility among them towards the conservation of their natural resources. This will also provide an opportunity for dialogue, consensus building and capacity development on climate change adaptation issues at community level.

The project will support the rehabilitation of the watershed as well as the promotion of soil conservation and stabilisation measures to reduce erosion and land degradation. The interventions in each area will take into account local socio-economic conditions, competitive ability, aspect and topography (slope stability and angle) as well as an assessment of the local surface

They are usually seeded between rows of crops or after harvest of the main crop and left to grow until they die down in the following season. Cover crops can be used as a means of reducing the sub-surface drainage and also eliminate soil mini-flooding. This sub-component will draw on significant in-country experience in recent decades. Rwanda's geography, demographics and climate variability have contributed to extensive land degradation and low soil fertility. This has created a need for sustainable land management practices that can be applied at scale.

Rwanda has over the years, developed significant experience and expertise in land husbandry techniques that reduce runoff, enhance soil water infiltration, and holding capacity on degraded lands enhancing the productive capacity of land and soil.

This has been supported with sizeable investments from the Government and development partners that have further deepened this knowledge. These include a range of soil conservation and land management practices including: radical and progressive terraces, check dams, soil water detention trenches, off cuts drainages, water harvesting structures, contour ploughing, intercropping, agroforestry, tree planting on degraded lands, establishment of permanent vegetation on contours, and agro-forestry and intercropping. These investments have yielded significant results in terms of increased agricultural productivity, improved soil fertility, reduced erosion and runoff upon which the productivity of farming systems depends. Several countries have already learned from Rwanda's experience and are now implementing similar practices.

The sub-component will establish protective forest on 1375 ha to stabilize slopes and reduce erosion. The Land Use Plan (LUP) for Rwanda indicates that slopes greater than 20% should be planted with trees, while slopes beyond 55%. On slopes steeper than 55%, the guidelines advise that land "should be used for permanent perennial vegetation". This sub-component will establish protective forest on 1375 ha of degraded lands. The establishment of protective forests is crucial to ensure that investments in erosion control and water management are sustainable.

### Sub-component 1: Stabilize rivers, ridges and steep slopes with protective forest (USD 1.5 million)

This sub-component will establish protective forest on 1375 ha to stabilize slopes and reduce erosion. The Land Use Plan (LUP) for Rwanda indicates that slopes greater than 20% should be planted with trees, while slopes beyond 55%. On slopes steeper than 55%, the guidelines advise that land "should be used for permanent perennial vegetation". This sub-component will establish protective forest on 1375 ha of degraded lands. The establishment of protective forests is crucial to ensure that investments in erosion control and water management are sustainable.

Protective forests** include deep-rooted species that can protect the slopes from slips. They help to stabilize soils and slopes disrupted by road building, as well protect men perches high from seasonal water flows. An important feature of these forests is their ability to retain soil moisture and reduce runoff. These forests also provide timber for fuelwood production, and a safety net in times of need. For example a protective forest on steep reserves can be used to harvest firewood at any time without fear of causing landslides or other damage.

Protective forests are established by planting trees on slopes with a wide range of species that can grow under different conditions. These include those including those caused by climate change. A detailed description of the studies** carried out as part of the preparation for this sub-component is provided in the Protective Forests, these are included in Annex I. This sub-component will establish protective forest on 1375 ha of degraded lands.

- **350 ha of steep slopes where plots are greater than 2ha
- **400 ha of steep slopes where plots are less than 2ha
- **80 ha on ridges and meadows and steep slopes on small plots <2ha

Protective forests are established by planting trees in a variety of ways (including under single or small group selection system) to retain permanent cover and avoid clear-felling. For plots sizes greater than 2ha, it is proposed to promote the planting of large diameter trees that can be harvested after about 15 years. On smaller plots, smaller willows could be established on steep slopes with a focus for species that provide smallholders with a wide range of benefits and services. The establishment of protective forests will provide a wide range of direct and indirect use goods and services. Tree planting is also an important tool in the fight against climate change as it helps to sequester carbon where indigenous or native species remain on the land. These shall be kept. Protect forest and other soil stabilization practices.

**A Protective forest can be defined as the long term establishment of trees on areas particularly sensitive lands to erosion or water runoff.

Sub-component 1.4: Integrate agro-forestry into farming systems on 970 ha of the watershed (USD 1.8 million)

This sub-component will establish agro-forestry on 970ha. A form of climate resilient agriculture, agroforestry® reduces soil erosion and increases organic matter, soil fertility and soil water holding capacity. It heavily relies on trees for its benefits. The project will establish a number of different tree species that increase deforestation which increases soil erosion, reduce soil quality and ability to retain moisture. It has additional benefits such as providing shade for crops, improving soil structure, increasing biodiversity and protection from sources. However, up-front costs and information are barriers to uptake which this component will address through the following activities: (1) Tree species selection; (2) Establishment of agro-forestry systems; (3) Boundary planting; (4) Trees scatted in crops; (5) Home gardens; and (6) Fodder banks. Trees and shrubs will be planted on existing land use areas, such as tea plantations, rice fields, fallow lands, pastures, croplands and/or overgrown slopes, and within crop and home gardens. Within the farm landscape, trees and shrubs will be planted in a variety of ways to provide shade for crops, improve soil structure, increase biodiversity and protection from sources. Trees and shrubs will also be used as windbreaks, shelterbelts, hedgerows, and/or barriers to reduce erosion. The project will support the establishment of agro-forestry systems on 970ha of land in the watershed. Specific investments will include:

* Planting trees/shrubs on existing radical terraces (non-tea fruit):370ha
* Planting trees/shrubs on tea plantations:500ha
* Boundary planting (non-tea fruit) 400 ha
* Establishment of agro-forestry systems on tea plantations:120ha
* Trees scatted in crops (tea and non-tea fruit)901 ha
* Establishing fodder banks with napier grasses 2937 ha
* Planting trees/shrubs within home gardens:548 ha

The project will also support Farmer Field Schools to promote soil and water conservation within agro-forestry systems. This will be done by establishing demonstration plots and workshops. Contour drainage ditches will be dug along the contours of hills to prevent water from running down the slope and causing erosion. These infiltration ditches will be established on 120ha of land in the watershed. The project will also establish a rainwater harvesting system, with water management and reducing soil erosion. More details and analysis are included in the watershed assessment report as part of the preparatory work. These are included in Annex 1.

Sub-component 1.5: Support 1800 small-scale crop-grow farmers to adopt agro-ecological approaches to increase climate resilience (USD 1.6 million)

Agroforestry (applied here by the community) used within context in Rwanda include: Atarai, Catua, Calabria Locustae, French Compressa, cinnara and Hikurawani.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 26 OF 93

This sub-component will support 1000 smallholders to adopt agro-ecological approaches (i) to increase climate resilience. Using agro-ecological practices including Integrated Soil Fertility Management (ISFM) has a number of benefits: (a) it increases soil moisture and water retention in soil, reducing crop vulnerability to short dry spells; ISFM is highly appropriate for smallholder farmers who are often limited by the availability of irrigation infrastructure; (b) it provides opportunities to use locally available nutrients sources more efficiently; (c) combining cropping systems with livestock can help meet household food security needs while also meeting other livelihoods needs with protecting the environment. ISFM also reduces dependence on oil imports and GHG emissions from burning fossil fuels for fertilizers. (d) It increases soil organic matter content, which improves water-holding capacity of the soil making farmers more resilient to prolonged dry seasons, improving crop productivity and ultimately increasing farm income.

### Climate-resilient vegetable production techniques including (SPF practices):

- Fodder crop establishment and conservation: and
- Zero-grazing systems: These are established for dairy households to ensure efficient manure production and improved livestock health.

Climate-resilient vegetable production techniques, such as kitchen gardens, mandala and double dug beds utilize water-and-waste nitrogen sources and use mulching techniques for moisture retention and soil fertility. Water is also conserved by using the rainwater harvesting system (RHS) to collect and store rainwater in tanks or drums outside of the traditional growing season increasing farmer resilience to drought and improving household nutrition security.

The project will be implemented through a community-based approach, with 300 Farmer Promoters which are trained facilitators who will work closely with smallholder farmers. The project will use Farmer Promoters to spread the use of improved farming practices. In total, 300 Farmer Promoters across 91 large and medium-sized communities will be trained in climate-resilient vegetable production techniques and assist smallholder farmers with climate-resilient approaches and techniques. The project will deliver ten days of training for each farmer promoter to ensure that they are equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge, helping them to develop training schemes, or facilitating training to demonstrate good farming practices for replicating in their communities. Farmer Promoters will also be supported by a facilitator who will provide technical support to the community demonstration sites (1 per sector) model; best practice adoption of climate resilient approaches and techniques will be demonstrated at these sites.

Farmer groups with an average of 30 members, Farmers will also receive limited inputs to support the implementation of climate-resilient vegetable production practices. The project will provide training for Farmer Promoters to assist them in selecting appropriate crops and varieties that are best suited to their local conditions, as well as providing training on techniques and technologies which will enable them to introduce production in a manner adapted to the effects of climate change. This will include training on how to use locally available resources such as manure and other base resource. These vulnerable smallholders will see improvements in farm production, food security and income levels over time.

The most vulnerable smallholder farmers will be identified and supported with additional agricultural and animal health inputs. The project will provide 1000 smallholder farmers with a total of 375,000 seedlings each (1,000,000 trees will be planted in total) to enable the local community to access fruit, food and timber resources that are currently not available.

The project will also support the establishment of farm and community demonstration sites to inform subsequent scale-up and field visits to other communities. The specific activities include:

- Building capacity of farmer promoters in climate-resilient techniques for smallholder farmers;
- Supporting the development of a training manual; and
- Establishing on-farm and community demonstration sites to inform subsequent scale up and field visits to other communities.

### Sub-component 1.E: Increase climate resilient farming practices with tea cooperatives in Mulindi (USD 2 million)

This sub-component introduces climate resilient agriculture to 400 smallholder tea growers to address the current weather-related risks and start preparing for future climate change at the Mulindi tea estate. This sub-component is implemented by the Tea and Coffee Resilience Study, which has been commissioned by the GCF.

There is one of Rwanda's largest tea plantations at Mulindi (in Gicumbi district), and this has been included in the project as it is a priority for the government to improve its productivity. The project will be implemented through two local co-operatives: (GODPETH and COATECH). The tea they produce is processed by a factory in the plantation, which is owned by the Rwanda Tea Board. The project is being implemented with support from the GCF Foundation and The Gatby Foundation, working under a shared ownership model with the two local co-operators (the small holders) at no consideration in the future.

A series of targeted low-regret adaptation interventions are proposed to reduce the exposure of smallholder tea growers to climate change. These include: reducing soil erosion; improving water management and irrigation; and reducing landslide risks in high risk zones. However, the project goes beyond this and aims to introduce a new planting system before the current one is exhausted, based on climate-resilient concepts. Investments include:

- Planting grass strips (crops grown) to reduce erosion and enhance soil establishment around the 1874 ha tea estate
- Establishing buffer zones of native vegetation in high risk areas
- Introducing shade trees in low risk areas (reducing sun over cherrypit areas while increasing water retention)
- Planting grass strips on terraces to reduce runoff and sedimentation

The design is supported by a detailed feasibility study and an analysis of the current and future climate risks? (see Annex 1). The analysis has used an iterative risk management approach to develop a portfolio of adaptation options that are low cost, low regret, and have high co-benefits. These interventions will be implemented in three priority areas: the tea estate; the surrounding catchment area; and the river basin. They align strongly with existing policies and have high national ownership. They will also provide key lessons for the future as Rwanda faces climate change impacts. The project is expected to generate significant co-benefits, including 30% of Rwanda's foreign exchange reserves and around 12% of exports by value. It is dominated by smallholders (45,000 ha) with a high degree of poverty.

The first set of activities will introduce low cost low regret adaptation around the plantation, with grass strips, tree planting, and buffer zones to reduce erosion and sedimentation while addressing climate change impacts that have occurred in recent years. Including the complete loss of 10 hectares of plantation due to land-slides in 2014. The project will also introduce a new planting system before the current one is exhausted as common as climate change is projected to increase heavy precipitation events in the area. These interventions will reduce soil erosion and sedimentation, improve water management and irrigation, and reduce landslides while also having wider ecosystem service and GHG reduction benefits. These interventions are community-based and will be implemented by smallholder tea growers with technical support from the project. They will contribute to improved water quality in the river basin, increased biodiversity in the ecosystem service and public goods benefits. Small scale 'green irrigation' will also be installed in an area of the plantation where it is currently not possible due to lack of infrastructure. This will reduce evaporation losses projected to increase under climate change. It will improve hillside gravity based irrigation and solar pumped drip irrigation, which are both more efficient than traditional methods. The project will also introduce shade trees on the river basin. Additionally, this selection of the existing planting (25 hectares) will be selected for planting as they have been shown to reduce soil erosion by 80% compared with the current system where erosion is reduced by higher maximum temperatures, noting that temperatures will increase under climate change.

The second set of activities will introduce low cost low regret adaptation around the catchment area and river basin. These investments are more transformational for the tea sector. There are plans to expand the Mulindi plantation, converting existing land from rice cultivation into tea plantations. This will be done by investing in the marginal green margin - on top of the existing baseline investment for expansion by The Expo Wind Foundation - to make this climate resilient. This will serve as the first example of climate resilient tea expansion in Rwanda. This is

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GROWING FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 28 OF 39

### The expansion of cultivation of tea (and coffee) to higher areas is a key transformative adaptation strategy for coping with climate change. The project will ensure that the areas for expansion are chosen carefully (ultimately leading to more resilient and sustainable production systems). This will be achieved by focusing on areas where the elevation is at or above 1,000 meters above sea level. At this higher altitude, the temperature is lower than in lowland areas, which reduces evaporation rates and increases soil moisture levels. The project will also ensure that the expansion of tea (and coffee) to higher altitudes is done in a way that minimizes environmental degradation.

### Sub-component 1.7: Integrate climate resilient practices into local extension services (USD 0.9 million)

This sub-component will strengthen the capacity of local extension services to support tea and coffee farmers with:

- **Farmer field schools (FFS)**, with Mulidu has two cooperatives (2 cops), 40 Tafas, 700 teashops 4900 farmers)
- **Farmer Field Schools** – with 2500 Coffee farmers in Goguma/Wamitama

Tea and coffee are the main cash crops for smallholder farmers in the district, using the farmer field school model. However, to date, these have not adequately covered the effects of current climate variability and future climate change on tea and coffee production systems. The project will ensure that the extension services are prepared for current risks of exposure to reducing losses from climate variability and are not prepared for the changing risks from climate change. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of local extension services through a field school curriculum, and developing new demo plots (field sites or field schools) that demonstrate the use of climate-resilient practices in tea and coffee production systems. The project will also ensure that there are two to six two-lesson operators. This will provide the training to allow farmers to introduce field interventions (complementing the current extension services).

The project will also support similar capacity strengthening for 250 smallholders growing coffee through existing farmer field schools in Goguma and Wamitama, with a focus on climate-resilient practices by these farmers. There are 17,783 smallholder farmers growing coffee in Goguma which is part of a large national coffee cooperative (Goguma Coffee Cooperative). The project will ensure that the extension services are prepared for current risks of exposure to reducing losses from climate variability and are not prepared for the changing risks from climate change. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of local extension services through a field school curriculum, and developing new demo plots (field sites or field schools) that demonstrate the use of climate-resilient practices in tea and coffee production systems.

Sub-component 1.8: Promote climate resilient practices for tea and coffee farmers to increase productivity and reduce losses from weather and climate variability (USD 0.6 million)

This sub-component will develop new weather and climate services (WACS) for the tea and coffee sectors, with their main focus on providing information about current and future climate conditions in the region, improving productivity and reducing losses from weather and climate variability, and have extremely high benefit to farmers.
