### cost-related). They include short-term forecasting (daily to a week) to help farmers (and government) with farm management activities (planting, ongoing management, harvesting, and post-harvest activities as well as climate services), including the development of early warning systems for pests and diseases. This component also includes supporting infrastructure and activities (climate data sets and monitoring, forecasting and prediction, information systems and tools, and capacity building). The focus is on developing a comprehensive system that can be scaled up to cover all crops in Ethiopia. This component therefore aims to build on this initiative and develop similar services for the tea and coffee sectors.

- Developing short weather and climate metrics of relevance for tea and coffee including in data sets;
- Undertaking participatory consultation on Mulusif with tea and coffee farmers in GamoGofa;
- Developing a seasonal forecast system (Mulusif);
- Building the capacity and monitoring infrastructure for met services to collect and process relevant information; and
- Disseminating climate services through SMS.

### Tea and coffee area perennial crops, there are many parts of the annual farming cycle that would benefit from better weather forecasting. This includes planting, flowering, fruiting, and harvesting (for example, application) and long-term data analysis at Mulusif has identified the key metrics that affect productivity, which yield the best results in terms of accuracy. The most important metric is the number of days with rain during the coffee flowering period. Weather forecasting is critical, as this period when coffee beans are dried (natural). In both cases, better weather forecasts will help farmers to make informed decisions about their crops and reduce the risk of property theft or damage, helping to reduce losses and protect livelihoods.

The project will develop a comprehensive system that can be scaled up to cover all crops in Ethiopia. This component also includes supporting infrastructure for smallholders - it will use a participatory-based approach with tea and coffee farmers in GamoGofa, combined with long-term climate data analysis at Mulusif. The project will provide the necessary tools and services to help farmers better understand their climate resilience, including a variety of products such as SMS to farmers. The services will also be incorporated into a farmer field school curriculum, and we will roll out this in the district (see component 1 above).

### For pest monitoring, surveillance and IPM to address current and future climate change (USD 0.6 million)

This sub-component targets the key climate relevant pests and diseases for the coffee and tea sectors, and develops a system of surveillance and early warning systems that can be scaled up to cover all crops in Ethiopia. Pest management as climate risks are increasing. Pests and diseases are already a major problem for both the tea and coffee sectors, and this is expected to increase with climate change. This will lead to increased crop damage. The climate is a major factor in the range and prevalence of these pests. Detailed analysis has found a clear relationship between rainfall patterns and pest pressure. For example, the number of days with rain during the coffee flowering period has been increasing in Rwanda, and increases significantly when the onset of the rainy season is delayed. A further increase in the number of days with rain will lead to an increase in the prevalence of coffee berry borer showing the range of key pests is increasing under climate change, notably with the coffee berry borer), which is now a major problem for Ethiopia.

- Surveillance and monitoring of targeted climate sensitive pests and diseases;
- Developing early warning systems; and
- Disseminating information to farmers.

The project will develop surveillance and monitoring of the key climate sensitive pests and diseases for tea and coffee, using survey information and GIS and statistical software to enable surveillance and forecasting. This will provide the necessary tools and services to help farmers better understand their climate resilience, including the introduction of IPM demonstrations in GamoGofa. See Annex 1 for more details.

### Sub-component 1.10: Increase climate resilient practices to reduce susceptibility to climate change and protect coffee to the increased GUDI (USD 5 million)

This sub-component will invest in climate resilient coffee development in Gicumbi. The project will work with the current coffee farmers in Gicumbi to increase their productivity, while also supporting them to plant a planned 35 ha expansion area climate-resilient in Gicumbi. This project will provide the technical assistance (climate risk management and adaptation) needed by the farmers to adapt to the impacts of climate change, demonstrating the required agro-ecological shift and transformative adaptation that will be needed under climate change. The project will also support the development of a new coffee variety in Gicumbi adapted to RAC15, which is resistant to major pests and diseases, as well as a further set of demonstration plots with varieties that are more resilient to climate change.

The project will also provide training on best practices for sustainable coffee production under a succession agronomy for coffee, which helps so to improve at the same time as enhancing adaptation. This approach will be based on the following principles: (i) planting coffee in areas where it is not currently planted; (ii) planting coffee on slopes; and (iii) introducing succession climate-resilient agriculture – 30% of Gicumbi.

### For Research and Development

The project will also support research to understand how to better adapt the sector to make it climate resilient. This is important as the Government is expanding coffee production, particularly for higher quality specialty coffee, so it may be necessary to change the current land use patterns in Rwanda. The expansion of coffee tea is critical that the shift of new expansion areas is made with the current rather than past climate in mind.

Arabica coffee requires very specific climate conditions during the growing cycle and it is a highly climate sensitive crop. It is also a high value crop, which makes it more vulnerable to climate change impacts. Arabica specialty coffee which fetches a higher price. Exposure to high temperatures (30°C and above) causes a range of physiological stress in Arabica coffee plants, leading to reduced yields and lower quality beans. The expansion of the annual crop. To address this challenge, Rwanda has introduced a high altitude variety of Arabica coffee called mountain arabica. This variety is more climate resilient than the current lowland variety of Arabica coffee (called wetter arabica) as much as 2°C per year over the next 30-40 years). This will change the climate of Rwanda and it will alter the areas suitable for growing coffee in the country. The mountain arabica is more suited to higher altitudes than the lower crop. These lower areas will therefore not be suitable for growing coffee (due to climate change). This is already happening with the current lowland variety, which is now being replaced by the mountain arabica.

Planting coffee on slopes is a well-established practice. While there are some short-term adaptation measures that can reduce short-term risks, due to the physiological temperature thresholds, there is no other alternative other than to plant coffee at higher altitudes. This will have several benefits: (i) it will increase yields; and (ii) it has many benefits as the coffee trees enhance soil conservation, therefore reducing soil erosion and flood/landslide risks. This is a critical adaptation measure that can be implemented now.

The project will also support research to understand how to better adapt the sector to make it climate resilient in the precipitation in the country (this coffee slope planting is a win-win option). Planting coffee higher up the slopes provides several benefits: (i) it will increase yields; and (ii) it has many benefits as the coffee trees enhance soil conservation, therefore reducing soil erosion and flood/landslide risks. This project will also support research to understand how to better adapt the sector to make it climate resilient in the impacts that climate change will have by reducing (current) production areas of Arabica. Moreover, slope planting at higher altitudes is a win-win option for coffee producers as it increases yields and reduces soil erosion. Coffee producing countries do not have any suitable higher areas and their production will fall. This means that Rwanda could be in the position to increase its coffee production by 2050. The mountain arabica variety is an opportunity here; therefore critical. Coffee also has another advantage, because coffee grows well on steep hillsides, which makes it a good option for smallholder farmers who have limited land and are looking for cost-alternative to more expensive terracing options). Coffee can therefore help with watershed management.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND WOODLAND FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 31 OF 93

This component will assist forest owners and users to improve forest productivity and reduce deforestation to create a wide range of forest products. The activities focus on reducing vulnerability to environmental change, increasing the resilience of forests, and enhancing their ability to provide high local capacity for sustainable development outcomes. These interventions are intricately linked with the watershed management component described in Section 2.3.1. As such, the design of these components will be interdependent.

The works will be concentrated on the West and South and forest productivity works to the East. The component is divided into two sub-components:

### Sub-component 2.1: Increase forest productivity and sustainable forest management (USD 1 million)

This sub-component aims to increase forest productivity and the uptake of sustainable forest management practices, including the introduction of new species and varieties that meet local market demands and needs.

The interventions strive for high-quality forest stands on a small targeted area. The design has been developed with the following principles: selection of appropriate species, provenances, hybrids or varieties, raised, planted, established and managed to professional best practice standards, supported by rigorous monitoring and evaluation systems. The introduction of new species will include Eucalyptus, which does not tolerate a sub-optimal practice; therefore, Eucalyptus plantations are only recommended for commercial purposes on sites that have been prepared with the appropriate soil preparation techniques or consolidated smaller pilot into larger blocks where owners can organize and operate together. Other woodyards, planted species will be introduced in small plots to demonstrate their potential as a new forest management practice (such as Phasai spp) and/or appropriate species for the production of other goods and services, with a particular emphasis on the introduction of new varieties and hybrids.

For the demonstration plots, a series of activities are proposed:

- Support the development of an adequate source of high-quality seed and plant material.
- Demonstration trial forest plots; and
- The phased roll-out.

The demonstration plots are an important and integral part of the phased roll-out of forest renewal.

The project will support the provision of nationally available sources of high quality seed and plant material. This will entail importing high quality seed and plant material (there is a lack of high quality seed and plant material in Tanzania). The project will also provide training to stakeholders on how to use this material, demonstrate and share awareness amongst stakeholders in nursery best practice and produce high quality seeds and plants. The project will also support the development of a forest tree nursery at Kalambo Forest (10ha) and target the district owned nursery, Kambaro in Nyaruguru village and the Muluzi Tea Factory. The investment will also support the establishment of a seed bank at the Kalambo Forest Research Station. This project has overall responsibility for implementing the recently developed National Trees Seed Strategy. Seeds stands (each 15ha) for each species will be planted to demonstrate best practice in nursery production and planting on the target areas to demonstrate and build correctly best practice.

The new species and varieties and best practices will be tested on demonstration trial forest plots. The trees will be monitored over a period of time to determine their performance and viability, and will not be limited understanding as to what productivity gains are achievable through the use of such material, and application of best practices in nursery production and planting. These demonstration trials will be established at Kalambo Forest (6.5ha); Gangwase forest in Kangla close to Muluzi Tea Factory (11 ha); Kiwira public forest in Mukungwa (14ha); and Kambaro Forest (20ha).

Forest renewal will be rolled out over 2281 ha of the watershed. This will begin with an assessment of the demonstration trial plots, followed by a detailed planning phase to determine the best species for each site. There is good potential for optimal growth and provision of forest goods and services. The renewal of forests will contribute significantly towards meeting national targets on afforestation and reforestation (2015-2030). Currently, Eucalyptus accounts for 88% of the project forest area. The current forest estate comprises 94.7%. Eucalyptus is a fast growing species that dominates the forest renewal design reduces this to 7%, increasing the use of other (larger native) species to 27%.

### Technical skills training for forestry technicians will entail the application of best practices standards and guidelines. The training will be provided by Mule Tea Factory, private forest owners, tree nurseries, tree growers, cooperatives. Capacity development activities will comprise a mixture of training, awareness and capacity building activities targeted to women and men comprising:

- **Technical skills training for 18 trainees**
- **Training of forestry core group (90 participants in total) and local community members**

### Sub-component 2.3: Establish, restore and manage degraded woodlots across 297 ha of the watershed (USD 8.6 million)

This sub-component will restore, restore and manage degraded woodlots with alternative species. Large-scale restoration is required to improve forest productivity and increase timber production in order to currently harvest sub-optimally at very short rotation lengths. This is primarily due to pressing needs to release cane to meet the increasing demand for sugar production and to maintain a sustainable supply of raw material for sugar and optimal revenue streams. It is therefore proposed that the project covers the area of improved woodlot management, which will be established in degraded woodlots across 297 ha of the watershed.

For small woodlots (c.5ha), the project will support farmers to enhance and diversify tree production by providing high quality seedlings, technical assistance for establishment and implementation of improved management practices.

### Sub-component 2.4: Strengthen community capacity to provide forest products and services (USD 1.4 million)

This sub-component aims to support income generation from forestry. Interventions were developed following a detailed assessment of the potential for small-scale tree nurseries, woodlots and beekeeping in Gicumbi! (see Annex I). It comprises a series of targeted interventions aimed at supporting livelihoods around forest products and services in degraded woodlots across larger areas. These include:

- Establishing community tree nurseries and capacity support:
  - Establishing tree nurseries;
  - Strengthening beekeeping operations; and
  - Strengthening the value chain.

The project will establish community tree nurseries and support the organisation, coordination and management of these tree nurseries and from other projects. However, there are few private nurseries in the target area and no established market for tree seedlings. The project will therefore provide a new nursery that will provide local communities with an additional source of income and diversify out of subsistence farming.

The project will also support the development of a value chain around woodlots and beekeeping operations.

### Small woodlot owners will be supported to form cooperatives.
This is intended to facilitate the aggregation and coordination of disparate and scattered woodlots and owners to provide economies of scale in operations, improve coherent marketing strategies for tree products and other forest products. The project will also support a wider range of product markets and improve planning and marketing opportunities for owners. This is because the majority of small woodlot owners are not aware of market opportunities or how to access them. With best practice technical support, owners will be supported to coordinate and manage their forest resources and access income-generating opportunities.

### Beekeeping operations will be strengthened to support livelihood diversification.
Beekeeping is an important local economic activity but is hampered by low productivity on honey and limited production of other

### Sub-component 2.5: Increase the use of biogas and efficient cookstoves to reduce deforestation of the natural forest

This sub-component will reduce the harvesting of wood for household cooking and energy use to reduce pressure on forest and wood resources, reducing vulnerabilities associated with deforestation and degraded land caused by fuelwood collection.

The project will target 10% of rural households in the area. The activities are based on extensive consultations and a review of existing work in this area in Rwanda (consultations held during the preparation of the National Forest Action Plan Districts Annex A). Activities will include:

- Promotion of the use of biogas by households engaged in dairy activities.
- Promotion and provision of cleaner cookstoves.

The project will benefit from the experience gained with Government programmes implemented by Practical Action Consultants (2011/12) and SNV 2013/14/23 under MINERFA and WEU/EDEC. These two projects have demonstrated that it is possible to promote the use of biogas in rural households, and that the production and installation of the stoves were developed by the Practical Action programme and adapted by the Rwanda Energy Group (REG) for local conditions.

The project will invest in buildings, some single machinery and support to a local cooperative to produce the stoves in Western Côte d'Ivoire and Eastern Ghana (in particular in the town of Bonyen in the Brong Ahafo Region) and in Western Côte d'Ivoire (in particular in the town of Yiriyen in western Côte d'Ivoire).

The project will also work with a number of key institutions in the district for improved cookstoves and the potential efficiency savings that could be achieved and provide 100 stoves to institutions. A MINIFER survey carried out by the University of Ghana in 2013 found that the average household in the country uses two or more pre-prepared meals, use the Muyen type stove made out of metal (80%) or both a stove made out of brick. Many of these stoves are not efficient and produce large amounts of smoke.

It is estimated that an institutional stove with a pan capacity of 150 litres, requires 200 kg of fuel wood per year. An improved stove will reduce the amount of fuel used by up to 70% which would result in a saving of 130 kg of fuel wood and also result in a saving of 100 m³ of firewood per newly installed stove. Further information can be found in the detailed TPI page on institutional stoves.

As part of the technical audit (described in section C.10), a series of efficiency measures have been identified that do not require significant modifications to the system and equipment, and will provide potential savings of around 35% leading to reduced emissions of around 4,600 t CO2e per annum as well increasing the overall energy efficiency of the plant.

### Sub-component: Manage surface water run-off from settlements to reduce guila formation, floods and landslides.

The interventions will target the most climate vulnerable households in Giocimo. The component is implemented by the Rwanda Water and Forests Authority (RWFA) – for riverine harvesting and surface water management activities; the Rwanda Housing Authority (RHA) - for housing development activities.

The implementation arrangements are explained in detail in Section C.7.

#### Sub-component 3.1: Manage surface water run-off from settlements to reduce guila formation, floods and landslides

This sub-component will comprise a range of measures to improve the management of surface water around settlements in order to reduce guila formation, floods and landslides.

### Measures to be implemented under this sub-component:

- **Reduce runoff:** Install check dams, diversion ditches, water breaks, soak-away infiltration pits, spill irrigation ridges, contour bunds etc. These structures will be planted partially along pathways and access roads, and along drainage channels to stabilize soils, reduce run-off and mitigate erosion.
- **Soil conservation:** Where possible, the soil cover in areas where guila formation is likely due to intense precipitation is exacerbated by rapid discharge from roofs, roads and other hard surfaces causing guila formation will be improved. This can be achieved through planting of grasses and shrubs on sloping land, as well as terracing.
- **Vegetation:** Vegetation will be planted in strips across the gully to slow the velocity of water, trip soil, and prevent further erosion. The vegetation will also help to reduce surface runoff by increasing infiltration rates. In the upper part of the study area and in the eroded area behind the branch gullies may also be required, such as retention and infiltration ditches, check dams, contour bunds etc., to slow down water flow and reduce the amount of surface runoff and direct surface water away from critical planned areas.
- **Soil conservation:** The soil cover in areas where guila formation is likely due to intense precipitation is exacerbated by rapid discharge from roofs, roads and other hard surfaces causing guila formation will be improved. This can be achieved through planting of grasses and shrubs on sloping land, as well as terracing.

### Investment details:

- **Check dams:** 100 check dams (324 each way per 1 ha) for 60% (96 ha)
- **Contour bunds:** 50 contour bunds
- **Gully pits:** Check dams: gully pits (180)
- **Terraces:** Terraces will be constructed in areas with steeper slopes (320 ha)

All work will be contracted through the cash for work schemes used elsewhere in Rwanda which increase employment opportunities and target the poorest households. Support will also be provided to establish cell water harvesting groups, as well as training for committees on surface water management techniques and rainwater harvesting (see below under 3.2).

### Sub-component 3.2: Increase reservoir capture and storage to counter inter-annual rainfall variability (USD 2 million)

This sub-component will invest in small-scale water collection and storage structures at the household and community level in upland areas to increase water conservation and reduce run-off and erosion during heavy rainfall. This will address the high levels of inter-annual rainfall variability in the district, and the periodic extremes (dry or wet) that are experienced by the communities. The project will also invest in small-scale water storage as a key strategy for climate change adaptation since using harvested rainwater will enable farming households to maintain their livelihoods during dry spells when there is no access to irrigation water from government schemes.

Rainfall harvesting enables households to manage their own water supply for drinking water, domestic use and livestock watering. It also provides an alternative source of income through the sale of produce and protection and allows more flexibility in market timing for farmers who produce market crops. Storing the water in ponds will provide a buffer against droughts and floods by reducing the risk of crop failure due to lack of water during dry spells, while providing irrigation water when needed.

Three main components are proposed: 1) small-scale water harvesting structures (ponds, dams, and ground contours) and 2) communal ponds. Roof top harvesting will entail using a subject class to assist women and men in installing rainwater harvesting systems on their roofs. The harvested water from the roof will be stored in tanks that will be shared between two households and spillguards will be used to prevent overflowing of rainwater. The project will also invest in small-scale water storage structures (ponds, dams, and ground contours) which will store water overflows and provide supplemental irrigation and water for livestock during the dry season (which will occur more frequently due to climate change). These ponds will be constructed using a subject class and will make increased use of biogas. Ponds will be made cost-effective by using local materials and community labour.

The design, materials, siting and installation of this water infra-structure will take the prevailing and projected rainfall patterns into account as well as other climatic factors such as temperature and wind speed located in each zone and in accordance with mitigating controls and regulations. Moreover, the project will ensure that the design of these structures is appropriate for the local soil type and climate conditions to ensure that they withstand heavy rainfall and other climatic extremes. This will include building in redundancies to ensure that the water infrastructure can still function even if one or more components fail due to extreme weather events.

The project will also invest in small-scale water storage structures (ponds, dams, and ground contours) which will be used to minimize the risk of stagnation. In addition, the construction of the ponds will be closely linked into erosion and flood control measures such as terracing, contour bunding, check dams, grassed waterways, vegetative slopes and riparian buffer zones, agro-forestry, check dikes, tree planting on gully banks, drainage channels etc.) to minimize soil erosion.

To ensure the sustainability of the small-scale infrastructures introduced by the project (ponds, waters breaks etc.), the project will establish a variety of committees at the sector and cell level to select the sites, manage the construction process, monitor the performance of the structures, and provide maintenance. It is expected that the committee will be responsible for the rehabilitation of the small-scale infrastructure after the end of the project. The project will establish effective programmes for training and capacity building in order to ensure that the committees are able to effectively plan, implement, maintain and eventually rehabilitate) of their services). The project will ensure that user participation is achieved through a participatory approach which includes community involvement at all stages from planning to monitoring.

The project will also develop a functioning management and maintenance system comprising tools, training, and resources for the committees. This will include establishing a database of water infrastructure projects to enhance the long-term technical, financial, and managerial capacity of the Committees to ensure the effective operation and maintenance of these structures. The project will provide training in areas such as: basic technical support for maintenance and repair; means for cost sharing of recurrent costs); supply to catchment and water users; management of small-scale infrastructure (e.g., construction, rehabilitation, operation and financial and administrative services etc.) and support for coping with ongoing climate trends and shocks including the development of a contingency plan. The project will also provide training in areas such as: basic operation and maintenance costs), define the roles and responsibilities of different players; and in particular build up the capacity of committees to ensure that they are able to effectively manage their resources, and establish committees to develop a mechanism for cost sharing to ensure investments are financially sustainable. The precise

### Sub-component 3.4: Community-based social housing development in Kabeza to reduce the number of vulnerable households living in high-risk areas (US$2 million)

The project will provide a range of interventions for vulnerable communities currently living in areas at risk from flooding and landslides. This intervention targets the poorest and most vulnerable households out of the 6,300 households identified as being at risk. The project will provide access to clean water and sanitation, adequate safe to water and poor sanitation. Masterplans have been developed for five rural sites in northern Ghana (Kabale, Kabeza, Bole, Togboli and Nsawam) that include a range of interventions including the provision of both sites as well as carbon sequestration calculations, options analysis, delivery and end plan, green infrastructure plan. The project will provide access to clean water and sanitation, adequate safe drinking water (sub-component 3.4). The settlements will include "green infrastructure" that utilizes renewable energy, water and waste management systems, and other technologies that are appropriate for the topography to reduce emissions, increase resilience to climate change and protect the surrounding environment.

Kabeza is a remote rural village on top of a hill, 7km from the border town of Gona, close to a well established settlement project. The Kabeza Village Project (sub-component 3.4) will be implemented as part of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) model village which was initiated under the rural settlement project in 2010 to upgrade rural settlements and provide access to basic services. The project will provide a range of interventions including infrastructure, services and amenities and to provide housing for vulnerable households living in high-risk areas with access to clean water and sanitation, adequate safe drinking water. 35 new dwellings in Kabeza were constructed in 2008 by a government agency. The village is located on the edge of the Bawku District to demonstrate best practice in sustainable rural settlement. The project will invest in the construction of 14 new dwellings, with each dwelling designed and built according to the local topography and building typologies and green infrastructure plan developed as part of the design. The new homes will be built on a raised platform to reduce flood risk and provide access for emergency services. The houses have views towards Russa town. The site is zoned as rural in the District Land Use and Zoning Plan and has no existing development.

The households in this village have already adopted a variety of green technologies including biogas, indoor solar lighting and climate adaptation measures including rainwater harvesting. A modified ICP materiality assessment was undertaken to identify the most appropriate interventions for the Kabeza village to promote enhanced climate resilience with gentle sloping, soft landscaping" to address wind loading, reduce soil erosion, improve water retention and increase biodiversity. The design will include a range of interventions that will help to reduce increasing risks of heavy precipitation from climate change. Each dwelling will include rainwater harvesting to cater for the increased rainfall expected in the future. A communal biogas plant has been designed as part of the project with each household having access to a communal renewable collection system will be constructed to collect water from the soft landscaping via a raised drainage channel. The design also includes a solar panel and battery storage system, which will power lights and sheos and two couches per household have also been included in the design. The masterplan also includes landscape features such as trees, shrubs and grasses that will help to reduce soil erosion and provide shade for livestock rearing terraces for food production.

The households will be provided with "infrastructure" (what utilises renewable resources, water and waste recycling strategies) at both sites are designed to work with the Bawkuand topography and climate to improve resilience. The existing house design currently used by Rwanda Housing Authority (RHA) in Kabeza is a two-bedroom unit that has been adapted for use on sloping land. The new design reconfigures the dwelling plot and existing four in one house design to adapt to the sloping topography up to 20% slopes. The new design also includes a raised platform, which will reduce wind loading and improve energy efficiency of structures. This design is more efficient in terms of energy and land use (make more land available for residential uses) and has been adapted to allow for expansion in the roof space into a future mezzanine that offer the potential for two additional bedrooms. The new design also includes a raised platform, which will reduce wind loading and soil stabilised brick structural piers (in place of high carbon structural concrete). The new design also has improved roof geometry for rainwater harvesting and an internal bathroom connected to a communal biogas chamber. Biogas will

enable households to cook without relying on fuelwood (either bought at high cost or illegally sourced from felling eucalyptus) and there will be improved ventilation in kitchens to deal with smoke from cooking.

Each house will have a kitchen garden and composting point to support household production of nutritious vegetables, fruits and herbs. The houses will also include a small livestock pen for poultry and goats which will be trained in low carbon construction techniques (Compressed Earth Blocks and Timber Roofing) and supervised by qualified trainers. The houses will be built using locally sourced materials and labour.

Where possible, the beneficiaries will provide the labour for the construction of their own houses. An incremental, cost-effective approach to building has been adopted which allows for the construction of a basic house with sustainable building materials and 'green' building typologies and technologies. Local people will also be engaged in providing training on low carbon construction techniques.

As well as providing shelter and access to services for 200 vulnerable families, the expanded housing project will create jobs through the provision of training and employment opportunities within the community.

The development of the site will be undertaken by the Rwanda Housing Authority (RHA). Throughout the construction process, RHA will provide technical assistance and supervision. The land on which the houses are to be built is located in the District of Gicumbi, who will donate the land to the GIP programme. Land title will pass from the District to the project once the houses have been completed.

The project will require a total investment of USD 10 million. This includes the cost of materials and labour which would be needed (including provision of training in construction skills) and is proposed under a wider call for TA funding by the GIP. The project will also require an additional contribution from the Government of Rwanda, which provides for the 'full financing' of basic infrastructure required, to and within the housing project area for housing development.

FONERINA and the District of Gicumbi will together oversee the allocation of the social housing according to the criteria described in the Cooperation Agreement between the FONERINA and the District. The Housing Project is being implemented under a Cooperation Agreement between the Rwanda Housing Authority (RHA) and FONERINA. RHA and Gicumbi District (as known as the PNU) will be responsible for providing technical assistance, supervision and monitoring of the project.

The District will provide details of households which have been allocated houses to raise the issue directly with the PNU in FONERINA.


### Criteria for Allocation

The District will allocate available housing units to deserving households relocated from climate vulnerable areas (high-risk zones) using the following criteria:

- **A**. Households assessed as extremely poor (in subcategory A1) which are female headed households and containing children under 5 years of age, pregnant women or elderly people.
- **B**. Other extremely poor households will be given the high priority; and
- **C**. Other extreme poverty households will be given the medium priority.

Where there are more qualifying households than houses available, the communities will select the beneficiary households in accordance with level of vulnerability. The District will provide details of the households which have been allocated housing units to raise the issue directly with the PNU in FONERINA.


### Detailed Cost Breakdown

A detailed cost breakdown of the development of climate resilient settlements has been provided in an earlier document (see Annex 1). Excluding the works supervision which covers both the housing and the communal green infrastructure, the cost per unit is USD 9300. This includes the cost for materials and labour required to construct a house with a total area of 45 m² which is equivalent to one room in an IHP house which is USD 8300. Although the cost is higher, there are considerable design benefits. The new design has improved roof pitch for rainwater harvesting and allows for improved ventilation in kitchens and composting point and an internal bathroom connected to a communal biogas chamber. ### Sub-component 2.4: Consistent green social housing development in Kenya to reduce the number of vulnerable households living in high-risk zones (USD 2.7 million)

This sub-component will implement a pilot project in Kigosi, located near the Mutha Tea Factory.

Kigosi is located near the Mutha Tea Factory. The site is well situated beside a loop road that follows the natural ridge with an average low density strip development of houses and only 2km from the Mutha tea factory and 4km to the main road. The site has a gentle slope, which will allow for construction on the lower slopes at a reasonable gradient below 20% and is suitable for development. There is a mainly electricity overhead connection with access to water supply and sewerage systems. The site is located within a mixed rural/urban context in the District land use and zoning plan but is a typical rural site, commonly found across the country.

The project will invest in 100 new homes and green infrastructure for vulnerable families currently living in areas at risk from landslides and flooding.

### Component 4: Knowledge transfer and mainstreaming (USD 3.7 million)

This component will ensure that climate resilience and green knowledge and learning from the project are transferred to other stakeholders, including local government authorities, farmers, and communities through strategies at the district and national level. These activities will include a range of activities to raise awareness, build capacity and share experience with others as well as establishing the enabling environment for replication.

This sub-component will analyse the results of the project and produce a range of knowledge products including policy briefs, case studies, reports, and other publications. - Develop case studies
- Competitions to reward innovative approaches to adaptation
- Webinars, webcasts, newsletters, radio
- Website
- Training and uptake of advice

### Sub-component 4.2: Awareness building, promotion and advocacy to support replication and scale up in disaster risk reduction

This intervention will build awareness and understanding of best practices approaches successfully implemented by the project to support the adoption and replication of successful approaches and technologies at scale in the communities. This will be achieved through a combination of workshops, training sessions, and classroom lessons learned. This will include building awareness of all project interventions including: watershed protection and management; water harvesting and storage; soil conservation and land use planning; and measures to increase resilience. This will be achieved through national and local workshops and the production of publications and other materials that highlight best practices in climate change adaptation, such as new materials, technologies and approaches to low carbon, climate resilient settlement development, the project will focus on building awareness at the community level about the benefits of these interventions for the current construction technologies and design and highlight benefits.

### Enhancing local capacity for climate change adaptation

Enhancing local capacity for climate change adaptation is critical in order to ensure effective climate adaptation. The project will target the training towards key staff in the local authority at District, Sector and Village level. This will include: District Officers; Sector Officers; Village Development Committees; and District staff this will include: Agronomists Office Environment Officers, Co-operative Operators, Infrastructure Officers, Water Resources Officers, Forestry Officers, Animal Health Officers, and Disaster Management Officers. The project will also organize seed and fertilizer distribution and provide extension advice. A capacity building programme will use project interventions to build the capacity of local authorities at District level to effectively manage many of the above functions dealing with aspects of rural infrastructure, land adjudication the restoration forestry and water resources management. This will be achieved through a combination of training workshops and by the 'primary focus' on agriculture, livestock and horticulture. Forestry Officers deployed at Sector level by MINAGRICULTURA will also receive training in climate change adaptation as part of their role. The project will target the Social Development Officers (better known as the Integrated Development Programme Officers or IDPO's) as this is the most important link between the local authorities and communities, and they are responsible for liaison with local and national NGOs.

### Training at the district level to build capacity up through a series of workshops

Training will be delivered at the national level to build capacity up through a series of workshops to promote best practices. Training will be delivered by project staff supported by technical specialists. The expected result is that District Officers, Sector Officers and Village Development Committees will understand how Government can best support communities to increase their adaptive capacity to future climate variability and an enhanced capacity to support community adaptation. ### Background Information on the Executing Entity

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 45 OF 93

**FONERWA was established by the Gulf in 2012 as a national fund based for climate and environment (FONERWA) to mobilise and manage the flow of climate funds across various sectors of the Rwanda economy. The Fund is governed by the FONERWA Law, which provides the legal framework within which climate finance is channelled, programmed, disbursed and monitored in Rwanda. The FONERWA Law formed the primary basis for establishing the fund and was developed with the support of the Government of Rwanda, the World Bank's International Climate Fund (ICF) and Gulf that contributed USD 4.3 million demonstrating its commitment to supporting climate change mitigation and adaptation activities in Rwanda. The Fund is supported by a trust fund for the NDA support and the strategic framework. FONERWA has a well-established operating system requiring significant resources, including personnel, systems, processes, and infrastructure to ensure that it delivers value for money, stakeholder engagement, and addressing gender and equity issues across cutting issues. To this end, FONERWA has leveraged USD 10 million in grant funding from the World Bank's International Climate Fund since 2013. FONERWA has leveraged over USD 3 million in private sector finance into LCCP projects since 2014.**

The Governance structure of FONERWA has been developed to allow oversight and Gulf control of its projects. The Prime Minister Orders (PMO) sets out the governance arrangements and gives powers to FONERWA to manage climate funds. The FONERWA Law provides for a Board of Directors which oversees the internal rules and procedures that guide the functioning of other levels including FONERWA Fund Technical Committee, Project Implementation Unit, and Project Management Units. Decisions on all matters are taken at each level. This project has been designed under the guidance of MCI and endorsed by a specially constituted steering committee. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government through climate resident inputs on the ground.

### Taxation and Insurance:
As a Government institution, MOE is required to follow the Revenue Constitution[1]and be liable for all national regulatory and tax framework. As the recipient of the GCF grant, MOE will therefore be liable to pay taxes on any income earned from the project. The same applies to FONERWA as it will earn income from goods and services sold; zero basis since they are for a project funded by a partner that has signed an agreement with the GCF.

In the design, any other tax liabilities will be borne by the government and have been built into financing plans and audit accounts and records of tax payments will be used to monitor the ongoing compliance with national tax laws. The same applies to FONERWA as it is a partner that has signed an agreement with the GCF and not a provider.

## C.7. Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

### MOE as Accredited Entity:
MOE is the Accredited Entity per the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed with the GCF in June 2016. The AMA sets out the roles, responsibilities, and obligations of the parties to the agreement. Under Article Per Particule of the Ministerial Order N°35/18 of 7/10/2016 Resting To Financial Regulations, all documents related to the GCF must be signed by the Minister of Finance and therefore MOE/CNCFIN will be a co-signatory party of the FAA.

Funds received by MOE under the FAA will be paid directly into a project bank account under the management of MGE.

### Implementation arrangements between AE, Executing Entity (EE), Gicumbi district and implementing partners:
MOE in its role as the Accredited Entity has the overall responsibility and oversight for the project including project implementation, quality assurance, monitoring, and project monitoring and reporting. It is responsible for:

- Listing with MINEPEN on the External Funding arrangement.
- Preparing a Project Implementation Plan (PIP).
- Chairing the Project Steering Committee.
- Preparing the Project Budget.
- Preparing project People plan and budget.
- Preparing the Project Monitoring and Reporting Plan.
- Preparing the Quality Assurance Plan.
- Preparing reports for annual progress reviews and final reports for projects which have received funds under the GCFRIP.
- Carrying out diligence on all entities which receive funds under the GCFRIP.
- Providing reports to the GCF on all of the above.

MOE will enter into a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with MINEPEN/CNCFIN to enable it to manage GCF requirements under AMA, FAA and Relevance. MoU and MINEPEN will perform a Quality Assurance role to ensure work done under the project is in line with the approved PIP and that all relevant documentation is completed as required by the project implementation. The quality assurance process typically deals with three competing dimensions of a project: technical, financial, and compliance. These are addressed through a three-stage process:

1) Develop a Quality Assurance Plan (to be updated annually).
2) Conduct an initial review of the Project Implementation Plan.
3) Conduct a final review at the end of the project.

The costs associated with the quality assurance activities will be borne by MOE, while the costs not related to the project budget. These will be covered by the AE Fee.

## Proposed governance structure of the project

The governance arrangements for the project at the national level will be designed to build upon the leadership provided by the Ministry of Environment, Energy and FONERWA (the executing entity). An inter-ministerial Project Steering Committee (PSC) chaired by the Permanent Secretary will be established by MOE to serve as the primary decision-making body. The PSC will consist of representatives from MOE, MOE/REMA, NIFRA, RHA, and other relevant stakeholders. The PSC will meet on a quarterly basis to review project progress and evaluations, facilitate implementation of the project, and provide guidance to the PMU. The Steering Committee will also provide technical guidance to the PMU. The Steering Committee will also facilitate effective coordination between the key implementing partners (MWEA, MOE/REMA, NIFRA, RHA, RECO, REMA, and other relevant stakeholders) in terms of project activities and programs.

The Steering Committee will reflect the multi-sectoral nature of the project, and will include senior level representatives from MWEA, MOE/REMA, NIFRA, RHA, RECO, REMA, and other relevant stakeholders. The PSC will be chaired by the Permanent Secretary (or the Director of Finance, MOE) with representation from NIFRA, Rwanda Housing Authority, REG, REMA, and other relevant stakeholders.

The Steering Committee will meet at least once a year to review project progress and evaluations, facilitate implementation of the project, and provide guidance to the PMU. The PSC will also provide technical guidance to the PMU. The Steering Committee will also facilitate effective coordination between the key implementing partners (MWEA, MOE/REMA, NIFRA, RHA, RECO, REMA, and other relevant stakeholders) in terms of project activities and programs.

FONERWA will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU) which will be housed internally within FONERWA. The PMU will be responsible for delivering the project and will be ultimately responsible for the timely implementation of all project activities as per the approved work plan. The PMU will consist of a Project Director, a Project Manager, and other technical staff to manage the day-to-day operations of the project. The PMU will also have a Procurement Officer and a Financial Management Specialist to administer the GCF funds and draw down on the technical assistance available through its contract with MWEA. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are delivered in accordance with the approved work plan, processes but importantly ensure strong fiduciary standards are adhered to and robust monitoring and evaluation is carried out as per the approved work plan.

The PMU will also have a Procurement Officer and a Financial Management Specialist - providing either full-time or part-time inputs including:

- Strategic Advisor
- Financial Management Specialist
- Procurement Officer

# D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

Rwanda is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change. The expected impacts on climate resilience and development are significant, as evidenced by the recent vulnerability analysis, high levels of poverty, a shortage of land and rapid population growth combined with limited access to resources. These factors will combine with other anthropogenic factors to significantly damage Rwanda's growth and development over time.

Rwanda has already made progress in developing its climate change adaptation strategies and is now ready to deliver the national strategies that have been developed by the Government.

As one of the poorest countries in the world, Rwanda does not have the necessary financial resources to address these challenges. The country has a strong focus on climate resilience and has set out the EOPES, the GCGCS and the NAPA. There is high potential to shift Rwanda to LCCR through the implementation of the proposed project.

Rwanda needs external financing to fund the additional net economic costs of climate change, and deliver its vision of a resilient country. The project will provide immediate benefits for the most vulnerable groups in society throughout Rwanda particularly those dependent on rain-fed agriculture. The upfront investment costs needed to implement the project are relatively low compared with the long-term benefits that can be achieved through these pathways. A GCF contribution is therefore crucial to build on the progress Rwanda has made so far and to take this project forward.

The proposed project will provide immediate benefits for the most vulnerable groups in society throughout development, GCF funding will enable the immediate roll out of proposed interventions, thereby i) enabling rapid development and ii) providing a strong foundation for future climate change adaptation. The project will support development against the GCF criteria and will be aligned with the priorities set by Rwanda's LCCR strategy (i.e., EOPES, NAPA, etc.), and national agencies (GPIID, KNR etc.) but it is ongoing and projected need for funding exceeds the capacity of existing sources of financing. The project will also support the implementation of LCCR, starting with the study commissioned by the Strategic Environment Institute (SEI) financed by DFID on economics of climate change projects that Rwanda will need to consider in order to achieve its national development goals and objectives.

Supporting the proposed project will demonstrate the fund's commitment to enabling developing countries, such as Rwanda, to implement their LCCR strategies. The project is designed to be a cost-effective intervention that can manage their own LCCR growth agenda. Evidence has already been presented by the Adaptation Funding Showdown that the GCF can provide a more effective and efficient way of financing climate change adaptation projects with agencies with quicker start up times and lower transaction costs, the underlying rationale for direct access arrangements. The project will also support the implementation of Rwanda's NAPA and EOPES on climate resource management; the fund can minimize its risk and maximize the impact of its funding.

The proposed project is designed to be a cost-effective intervention that can manage their own LCCR growth agenda with active participation and designed to a cost effective way to ensure sustainability of outcomes. Supporting climate resilient land use, forest and natural resources management will provide immediate benefits for the most vulnerable groups in society throughout the project. The innovative approaches put forward will also showcase what is possible and the knowledge management component will support the scaling up of successful approaches. Directly financing MOE (and hence FONERVA) also means that expertise development can be provided to Rwanda's national agencies, which are already well established and widely used and is already mandated to coordinate climate finance at the national level. The proposed interventions will meet all GCF funding profiles and meet the minimum criteria as set out in Section 6.

The project will model deliverables in local performance contracts, budgets, procurement and development plans to ensure that all parties involved are aligned on the same goals. This ensures project participants (district authorities, local communities, contractors etc.) have a shared vision of outcomes and success criteria. The project will also use the participatory approach which has been widely accepted throughout Rwanda and has been shown to be an effective means of delivering results. The project will ensure that all parties involved are aligned on the same goals by developing plans ensuring resources are properly allocated and administered within existing systems and structures.

The financial structure of the project and the individual interventions have been designed to ensure long-term sustainability, with a focus on building capacity for climate resilient agriculture, forestry and land husbandry etc. This will be achieved through the development of local institutions that can continue to operate after the end of the project period in the long run beyond the Fund intervention period, and in cases where demonstration interventions have been selected as part of the project, these will be scaled up by the communities involved.

The strong emphasis on local capacity building will create the skills and expertise needed to shift to adaptive management practices. This will be achieved through the development of a community-based climate resilient agriculture, forestry and land husbandry etc., along with providing opportunities to use existing resources in new ways that are more sustainable and better adapted to changing conditions.

The project will also ensure long-term sustainability by focusing on the over-cultivation of marginal lands in the larger areas which exacerbates and contributes to unsustainable agricultural practices. This will be achieved through the development of local institutions that can continue to operate after the end of the project period, and in cases where demonstration interventions have been selected as part of the project, these will be scaled up by the communities involved.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 53 of 93

## E.1 Impact Potential

### E.1.2 Mitigation impact potential

The project will have a significant impact on adaptation capacity and resilience among vulnerable groups as well as increased awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.

The main adaptive benefits will include:

- Improved soil and water management, forestry and agroforestry which reduce erosion and the reduced vulnerability to droughts.
- The project will enable 750 people dependent on rain-fed cultivation of marginal lands to transition to diversified, climate-resilient crops such as coffee, tea, cocoa, and bananas.
- Highly vulnerable smallholder farmers reaching 1800 smallholders, 4900 tea farmers and 2500 coffee farmers in Oromia region will benefit from the project. The project will also provide training on climate-smart agriculture use of climate and weather information decision-making by farmers; the project will also build resilience and enhance adaptive capacity among vulnerable groups through improved access to climate services, including farmers' major source of exports tea and coffee (which represent 25% of all exports by value) and their ability to adapt to climate change.

Mitigation benefits are expected in several sectors and so also have high impact potential. Investments in energy efficient technologies for cooking, and increased efficiencies in its factories will result in significant reduction in carbon emissions from the sector. The reduction in carbon emissions from the livestock sector can be achieved through improvements to the operating system of its facilities across Rwanda, with energy efficiency practices and improved feed management. The project will also contribute significantly to the national effort in the NAMA for reducing current direct industrial emissions and the cost-effectiveness of options to reduce emissions in the sector.

The project will have a significant impact on adaptation capacity and resilience among vulnerable groups given the widespread use of inefficient cooking methods using wood and charcoal, with expected emission reduction from the sector. The project will also contribute significantly to the national effort in the NAMA for a significant reduction in emissions (70,061 tCO2eq from agroforestry and 49,881 tCO2eq from forests) over the period covered by the project.

The total emission reductions are expected to be 35% of the baseline (calculated at year E) as a result of these interventions; thus the total emission benefits of the project is much larger than this. In all cases, the cost-effectiveness of these interventions is high, as presented in Section EE.1.

## E.2. Paradigm Shift Potential

Describe how the proposed activity can catalyse impact beyond an off-project investment

### E.2.1. Potential for scaling up and replication (Provide a numerical multiple and supporting rationale)

The district-level interventions are expected to be highly replicable in Gipuzcoa due to varying degrees typical of other districts in Rwanda increasing the transmission of successful practices. The potential exists to scale-up and replicate these interventions across Rwanda, particularly in areas where there is high demand for land management services. Proposed interventions aim to reduce risks from flooding and landslides on forested land and scale up in other districts across Rwanda. Channeling funds through Rwanda's national fund will enhance scaling-up of capacity building and institutional strengthening while also compatible development in Rwanda while maximizing opportunities for further scale and replication.

Channeling the funds through Rwanda’s climate finance architecture FONERWA also means that there is significant potential to build on existing synergies with other ongoing projects. This includes identifying linkages and synergies with existing and planned climate relevant investments and actions for the efficient delivery of mitigation and adaptation activities in a manner that maximizes impact and minimizes duplication.

A competent national institution, GCF will enhance existing institutional capacity and facilitate improved coordination of development efforts across sectors and stakeholders. FONERWA is a multi-sectoral institution which coordinates a series of demand driven investments into climate adaptation and mitigation around the country and has a strong track record in coordinating such activities.

There is strong potential to cost effectively scale up, replicate and sustain the results of the project interventions. The proposed activity will build on existing synergies with other ongoing projects while also building towards existing types of development projects which, while beneficial, will not deliver the paradigm shift which is the goal of this project. This will be achieved by focusing on a limited number of key institutions and targeted investments towards the key institutions that are driving the development of the respective sectors and sub-sectors in Rwanda. The proposed activity will also build on existing synergies with other ongoing projects.

The investment in building capacity and mainstreaming will support the ongoing replication of adaptation and mitigation activities across all sectors and stakeholders, including those not directly supported by the Green Growth and Climate Resistance Strategy. As the national fund climate, FONERWA is also uniquely placed to respond to the needs of the various sectors and stakeholders as they develop their respective pipeline of projects that are under development. Importantly, the mainstreaming approach adopted will ensure that the capacity building and institutional strengthening activities are embedded into project plans, such that the interventions and information is embedded into policy and will deliver the paradigm shift.

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDFALL FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 57 OF 93

Forestry and watersheds: Forest coverage and woodlots in Gicumbi district are similar to those in other parts of the country and the best practice forest management methods, efficient cooking technology and the associated income-generating activities have been widely adopted. The project will build on these successes by supporting the farmers in Gicumbi who are expected to demonstrate what best practice can achieve and combined with the capacity building and re-negotiation of the Forest Investment Round 4, this is expected to create an irreversible shift to improved forest management in other parts of Rwanda.

Tea and coffee: These are Rwanda's most important cash crops and support thousands of smallholders across the country. The interventions have been designed to address key barriers to adoption, including information and market access. The project will provide training on good agricultural practices (GAP) for tea farmers and deliver at scale. In addition to the 740 farmers benefitting directly, an estimated 357,735 indirect beneficiaries will also be reached through the dissemination of knowledge and best practices. The project will support the development of a climate-resilient export crop that creates a production shift, and has the potential for a transformative effect, moving beyond the current focus on coffee and tea. The project will provide training to 100 farmers in each of the 15 tea districts in Rwanda is expected to have a huge impact on GHG emissions because the baseline emission factor for tea is 2.3 times higher than that of coffee. The project will also support the development of key institutions, NAFORI and RARI during this expansion phase will enable many of the innovative approaches to be scaled up across Rwanda. The project will provide training to 100 farmers in each of the 15 tea districts in Rwanda, also enables successful approaches to be easily adopted, replicated and scaled up onto other tea estates owned by cooperatives or private companies. The project will also support the development of a climate-resilient coffee crop that creates a production shift, and has the potential for a transformative effect, moving beyond the current focus on coffee and tea. The project will provide training to 100 farmers in each of the 15 coffee districts in Rwanda. This will enable successful approaches to be easily adopted, replicated and scaled up onto other coffee estates owned by cooperatives or private companies. The project will also build on the Wood Foundation's philanthropic plan also create additional opportunities for scaling up climate-resilient crops across Rwanda.

Settlements: The settlements in Gicumbi district are simple and single-story and are easily replicable and transferable and therefore have strong potential to continue beyond the end of the project. Extensive deposits of clay exist throughout the country, which can be used as a construction material for building houses that can be replicated across the county to meet the high demand for building materials. The housing typologies and green infrastructure are simple and replicable at scale. The settlements in Gicumbi district are climate-resilient construction are simple and can be easily replicated and scaled up country-wide. The improved surface water management practices developed through this project can also be applied in other parts of the country and will be promoted and mainstreamed under component 4.

A full Theory of Change which illustrates the inter-locking roles of the four components is provided in Section C.

The project includes measures to transfer and mainstream the knowledge generated and lessons learned from previous projects, including:

- **Establishment of a forest nursery and forest management demonstration sites:** These are all located along roads and easily accessible. They will be used as training venues for farmers and other stakeholders.
- **Forest management demonstration sites:** These are all located along roads and easily accessible. They will be used as training venues for farmers and other stakeholders.

### Forest stakeholder dialogues:

The project will support the development of forest stakeholder dialogues in each of the 15 tea districts, including key decision makers (including forestry staff and professionals) emerging results through these dialogues can be shared with other stakeholders. The project will also support the development of soil erosion reduction and landslide control to create a systematic evaluation of success factors and challenges to scaling up climate-resilient crops across Rwanda. The RACF investment will support a range of other activities to support learning and knowledge development including:

- **Production of knowledge products:** Including policy briefs, guidelines, extension materials etc.
- **Training and capacity building:** For farmers, forest officers, and other stakeholders.
- **Gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation system:**

### Awareness building of new approaches and technologies with farmers as well as local planners and policy makers at the national level.

These measures will strengthen the knowledge and capacity base for climate action and facilitate dialogue for an effective climate response at the national level. The proposed investment in knowledge development, communication and awareness building is timely because there is real potential to create new markets in low carbon industries and for this project to be transformational.

Awareness building will also ensure that knowledge and information are shared widely, building wider support for climate action and creating a conducive environment for the uptake of climate-resilient approaches. The learning will also provide for continuous feedback on impacts and results at the community level. The integrated approach will maximize learning and produce more robust and sustainable results than a fragmented approach that would not be able to address the change. That fact the project is consciously and deliberately designed to address interlinked sectors, vulnerabilities and barriers to climate action makes it particularly relevant as many other projects fail to address underlying barriers or related issues undermining delivery of results. Thus by seeking to address not only climate change but also development challenges in a holistic manner, this integrated design tool provides a greater assurance of the sustainable delivery of benefits and impact.

#### E.2.3. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The GCF investment will support the implementation of the project activities that are designed to create an enabling environment for climate-resilient approaches and technologies at the local level, including policy reforms, capacity building, awareness raising, knowledge sharing, and technology transfer. The project will also contribute to the development of a comprehensive framework for mainstreaming climate change into national policies and strategies.

The GCF investment will strengthen the technical capacity of key government agencies, local NGOs, cooperatives, and other stakeholders at the local level in order to enable them to effectively plan, implement, and respond to climate change. Strengthening sectoral and local-government agencies will raise awareness and understanding on climate-resilient approaches and technologies among policy makers and decision-makers at the national as well as the local community. The project also targets barriers to the uptake of climate resilient approaches. There is a strong emphasis in this project on building capacity for mainstreaming climate change into sectoral policies, strategies, and development at the local level which takes account of climate change during and beyond project activities. Building capacity will be done through training workshops, seminars, and other learning events.

The GCF investment will foster the integration of climate-resilient approaches and technologies into key areas of the local economy creating a conducive environment for the uptake of climate-resilient approaches and technologies at the local level. The project will also contribute to the development of an enabling environment for climate change delivery that will facilitate the uptake and mainstreaming of climate analysis and successful approaches at the national level.

## E.3 Sustainable Water Management Potential

### E.3.1 Environmental, social and economic co-benefits including gender-sensitive development impact


The Project is expected to generate a range of environmental, social and economic co-benefits.

**Economic co-benefits:** The promotion of climate-smart agricultural practices will enable target households to address current climate threats to rain-fed agricultural production and improve food security. This will be achieved through the adoption of improved crop varieties, better water management techniques and increased use of efficient irrigation systems. These interventions will benefit people and includes a strong emphasis on financial and environmental sustainability to secure long term livelihood opportunities for smallholder farmers. The project is expected to generate significant economic co-benefits with positive net present value, demonstrating the economic rate of return (high) as seen in Section E.6.3). For these components, the project will be financially self-sustaining over a 20-year period and the investment is high and demonstrates their financial viability in the long term. Other economic co-benefits include:

- Increased income from agricultural production opportunities;
- Reduced costs of water management;
- Reduced costs in production due to stress on reduced health care costs for the sector; and
- Reduced loss and damage costs associated with impacts, response and rehabilitation costs from extreme weather events.

**Environmental co-benefits:** Proposed interventions to restore degraded habitats and improve the management of natural resources will contribute to the restoration of ecosystem services. These interventions are expected to reduce pressure on climate–resilient technologies and approaches that is expected to enhance ecosystem services and agricultural productivity in the long term. The project will also contribute to the rehabilitation of degraded waterbodies (through reforestation, agro-forestry, erosion control, promotion of efficient livestock etc.). This will be achieved through the adoption of improved crop varieties, better water management techniques and increased use of efficient irrigation systems.

**Social co-benefits including gender-sensitive development impact:** Proposed interventions target some of the poorest and most vulnerable households in Rwanda and are expected to generate significant social benefits for local communities. The increased water and energy security resulting from improved watershed management and other environmental co-benefits include:

- Reduced pressure on forest and land area resources for the provision of timber and non-timber products;
- Improved soil quality, water retention capacity and increased agricultural productivity; and
- Increased access to clean drinking water.

The project will also contribute to the promotion of efficient irrigation systems.

## E.4. Heads of the Recipient

### Vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population
#### Adaptation (Modification) Only

Overall, the project will address the vulnerability and financing needs of the country and its population as outlined in the previous section. The following table provides an overview how the project addresses the issue as a whole.

The vulnerability and financing needs of country and its population are evidenced by the recently prepared national climate vulnerability index. The study indicates the scale and intensity of vulnerability of Rwanda to climate change, with the highest vulnerability being experienced in the Northern Province (national survey) of household vulnerability. It reinforced earlier studies that found that the Northern Province where the project is located has a high level of vulnerability to climate change. The Eastern Province (the most sensitive to soil erosion and landslides due to its mountainous topography and steep ravines) also has a high level of vulnerability, as does the Southern Province (where there were 10 deaths between 2011 and 2012 alone). In 2013, there were 3 deaths and 52 houses destroyed or damaged by landslides. The largest number of people affected by landslides in 2013 was in the Southern Province (Rutana, Muyinga and Muramvira).

Poverty levels are also very high compared to national levels, with 55.3% and 67.7% of the population living below the poverty line or in extreme poverty respectively (ESCA). The project specifically targets vulnerable groups such as women, youth, the disabled, and other vulnerable groups who are often excluded from the risks that exist between men and women particularly in rural Rwanda and ensure that these benefits are distributed equitably. These groups include small scale farmers (with 72% of the district's population engaged in small scale farming) and where there are limited access to land and resources, especially for women and youth. The project will also target vulnerable groups among young children and food insecure (44% of children in Gicumbi have chronic malnutrition). The high levels of poverty and vulnerability are exacerbated by the fact that Rwanda is one of the most vulnerable countries globally to climate change and extreme weather events. The poorest groups are most affected by climate change, with 70% of the population living below the poverty line or in extreme poverty (ESCA) and are more likely to suffer from disaster and climate change impacts. The project includes interventions to restore ecosystem services, strengthen community resilience, improve access to land and resources for vulnerable groups, increase income generating services and products to increase household incomes and strengthen livelihoods so that vulnerable communities can better cope with the effects of climate change.

The project will also target areas where there is limited access to water. The lack of reliable water availability will have a direct impact on smallholder production, enhancing food security for the district's poorest and most vulnerable households.
