A recent risk assessment by MIGSARM also confirmed that the country is highly prone to drought, landslide, flood and windstorms. Because of its geographical features (the island is hilly and mountainous with an average elevation of about 2000m above sea level), Rwanda is particularly vulnerable to landslides, floods, heavy rains and heavy winds, thunderstorms and lightning. The cost of the disaster response and recovery in recent years has been very high. In 2013 alone, the country experienced a series of disasters with the main cause of flooding across the thirty districts being climate change which results in heavy rain and flash flooding through rivers and streams. Rwanda is also highly vulnerable to droughts due to its geographical features and landlateral from moderate to very high slope susceptibility with 41% of the exposed population being children aged <20 years old. The country has a total of 35 districts, out of which 27 are mountainous and 8 are hilly. The area estimated to be exposed to landslides is 69%. In terms of infrastructure, 43% of Rwanda’s health facilities, 25% of its schools, 10% of its roads and 10% of its bridges are located in mountainous areas.

The country has a high dependency on agriculture for employment (87%) and food security. The vast majority of national production is exposed to landslides with the economic cost of damaged houses due to landslide estimated at USD 25 million per year. In addition, the country’s water resources are highly vulnerable to climate change and scarcity. Rwanda is classified as a water scarce country under high water stress. Around 40% of Rwanda’s land area has been degraded by soil erosion and deforestation. The country also faces challenges in food security because before cultivation, only 23% of the land is not prone to erosion. With over a half of Rwanda’s power derived from hydropower, the country is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts on water resources. In MW in 2014 it was 141.97 MW by 2018[6], reducing its erosion and protecting the country’s watersheds is critical to renewable energy and food security.

### E.4.3 Financial, Human and Social institutional needs

Describe how the project addresses the following needs:

- Absence of alternative sources of financing (e.g., local or federal payment) that presents for addressing the needs of the country.
- Lack of capacity in the public sector to implement the project.
- Capacity constraints in the private sector.

Section 1.0 provides a detailed overview of the economic and social development level of the country which has been summarized below:

Rwanda is one of Africa’s poorest countries with Official Development Assistance (ODA) financing approximately 40% of the country's annual budget. The country is small, hilly, and landlocked and has the highest population density in sub-Saharan Africa. Rwanda is a landlocked country with no access to sea ports and generates more than 45% of the country’s export revenues (mostly from tea and coffee). Rwanda has a high dependency on agriculture for employment (87%) and food security. The industrial sector is constrained by a lack of locally available raw materials and high transport costs as a result of being landlocked. The agricultural sector is also constrained by low productivity due to the smallholder population engaged in rain-fed, subsistence agriculture and a high dependence on biomass for fuel, there are constraints with respect to access to credit and inputs. Rwanda has a mountainous terrain (the average surface has slopes greater than 10%, which results in high levels of erosion and surface run off into waterways). The country is highly vulnerable to droughts due to its geographical features.

Rwanda lacks the financial resources to adequately address climate threats and support low carbon growth. Rwanda’s total public expenditure on climate change adaptation, mitigation and disaster risk reduction outlined in the GCRIS have been estimated to be around USD 2.7 billion (cumulative to 2030) compared to business as usual scenario of USD 15.8 billion. The cost of implementing the LCCP for the implementation of LCCR development activities in Rwanda is mobilised from four principle sources:

- domestic revenue mobilization
- external development partner resources,
- international climate funds, and
- domestic sector resources (e.g., NGOs, private sector).

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 62 OF 93

The fiscal balance for Rwanda is inadequate to finance the additional costs of adapting to climate change and scaling up green climate development pathways outlined in the GGGRS. In the medium term, total revenue and grants are projected to be RWF 1,740 billion (28% of GDP) in 2016/17 and RWF 1,744 billion (31% of GDP) in 2017/18. Total expenditure is projected to be RWF 1,741 billion (28% of GDP) in 2016/17 and RWF 1,995 billion (30% of GDP) in 2017/18. Foreign reserves coverage at the end of 2014 was 1.4 months of imports. The country has limited access to international capital markets because it has limited experience of capital bond markets although a regular bond issuance program was initiated in 2014. This means that Rwanda is heavily reliant on external financing for its development needs and therefore requires ongoing support from donors to plug its fiscal gap. This project will make a direct contribution to Rwanda’s ability to finance its climate change adaptation activities.

Certain agencies including the Districts and MOE receive relatively low levels of budget support and the limited availability of private funds hampers the need for additional external finance. Strong alignment among these agencies with the GGGRS, the National Development Strategy (NDS), and the mainstreaming of Climate Compatible Development (CCD) into planning and budgeting but further mainstreaming such activities in all sectors is critical to ensure that climate change adaptation investments are made. Such investments, are important steps towards sustainably financing LCRG growths in Rwanda. Moreover, attracting private sector investment for climate change adaptation projects remains a challenge due to the high interest rates and risk aversion finance institutions unwillingness to finance investments in areas with limited market demand, inadequate infrastructure, ambiguous tax rules, and a lack of skills in the workforce. Given the relatively new initiatives on climate change adaptation, there is a need to build capacity within the public sector and private sector agents will require new skills and knowledge to be able to establish and deliver these goods and services. The project will contribute towards building such capacities.

The Government of Rwanda (GoR) has recognized that it needs to improve its ability to manage climate change adaptation investments in order to ensure that they are effective, efficient and sustainable. The GoR can play a crucial role in helping Rwanda to overcome these financial limitations and achieve its goal of climate change adaptation.

Rwanda has demonstrated success in terms of its institutional capacity to absorb and manage climate finance. However, the country still faces challenges in managing climate finance effectively. Despite Rwanda’s good progress towards fiduciary management standards, its commitment to service delivery and its strong performance on the World Bank’s Country Policy and Institutional Assessment (CPIA), it continues to face resource limitations.

The establishment of the FONERWA provides a robust structure for channeling, programming, disbursement and monitoring climate finance. The FONERWA is a multi-donor trust fund established by CSOs and the private sector. The fund has also established a concessional lending facility for eligible private sector entities to access low-interest loans at 5% per annum. However, despite its good performance on the Rwanda context, as national financial institutions view environment and climate investments high in nature, often they are not willing to provide financing or even to engage with the FONERWA.

The lack of a clear, costed implementation plan means that the project is constrained by limited capacity at both the national and sub-national levels resulting in a limited capability to implement national policies and plans. The lack of a clear, costed implementation plan also results from the fact that the economic context of EPRIS is proving challenging due to its crosscutting nature and because responsibility for climate change adaptation is shared across multiple sectors. This has led to significant challenges with complicating issues of ownership, monitoring and coordination. The lack of a cost, clear, costed implementation plan means that the project is constrained by limited capacity at both the national and sub-national levels resulting in a limited capability to implement national policies and plans.

Public agencies require additional funding, technical support and capacity development in order to capture climate change adaptation opportunities and to mainstream climate change considerations into many of the existing and planned operations. Proven approaches for which knowledge, systems and skills are already available include: (i) building institutional and management system capacity; (ii) providing individual staff, institution and management system development and support as it becomes the new business as usual in Rwanda; (iii) ensuring that climate change adaptation is mainstreamed into all sectors at levels below the highest levels, particularly in Districts. Effective mechanisms are needed to ensure good communication and flow of information between the central government and local authorities, including District institutions and organisations at District and sub-District levels. Financial and human capacities are overwhelmed which impedes the translation of climate-resilient and low carbon concepts and activities endorsed nationally into

E.5 Country Ownership

### E.5.1 Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAMAs, NAPs and NAPs

Rwanda is recognized worldwide for its commitment to poverty reduction and environmental development, and the NAP is seen as an important tool in this regard.

The Rwandan government recognizes that climate change is recognized worldwide and there is strong ownership of the development and green growth agenda. Rwanda has established a strong and effective policy framework for addressing climate change. The National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) is one of the key policies and legal frameworks are in place including a national climate fund which can absorb, disseminate and monitor climate change information. The NCCP was developed with the aim to mainstreaming the effects of climate change and Rwandan institutions, particularly MINIRENA have good experience from implementing projects that address climate change.

The Government has also established a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) in 2015 which aims at reducing carbon growth in its policy framework across Government and has made significant efforts to build awareness on the impacts of climate change. Rwanda has also developed a national action plan after the genocide against the Tutsis, create small woodlots and agroforestry have increased cover.

NAPs:

The planned interventions are closely aligned with Rwanda's national priorities for low-emission and climate-resilient development. The NAP is designed to ensure that all sectors of the economy adopt compatible development priorities as it is closely aligned with national strategies and policies. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) will be developed in close collaboration with the Government, taking into account vulnerability to climate change particularly from the main climate hazards including intense rainfall, flash flooding, landslides, droughts, heat waves, cold spells, and extreme temperatures.

The NAP will include four adaptation options to climate change, four of which are included in this project: 1) promotion of 'non-agricultural income generating activities' (NIGAAs), including livestock production and other non-farm sector threats; 2) Integrated Water Resource Management via the watershed protection and sustainable forest management, with a focus on protecting key species tolerant to climate variability; and 4) development of alternative sources of energy to low-emission.

NAMAs:

Rwanda submitted its NAMA to the UNFCCC in June 2015[16]. The GGR established an institutional, legal and policy framework for Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMAs). In May 2014, a series of NAMAs have been developed by the Government. These include: 1) land use change and forestry; 2) agriculture, livestock, forestry, transport, and waste to address sustainable development in the context of mitigating GHG emissions beyond the Kyoto Protocol commitment; 3) energy sector to reduce energy intensity and increase relevant sectors to this proposed project are 1) land use change, and forestry (LULUCF), 2) industry.

Including tea and coffee) and bi) buildings. The NAMAs for each of these are presented in the following paragraphs.

The most pressing challenge in land use, land use change, and forestry (LULUCF) is the current and continuing deforestation of Rwanda's forests, which has led to a decline in forest cover from 30% to less than 15%, increasing the basic productivity of Rwanda's forests and increasing the supply of wood through improved forest management. The proposed NAMA includes forestry programs (see Table 1). The proposed project includes provision for all six shortlisted scenarios:

- Improved management of forests.
- Improved land use.
- Forestry business (tea, wood, and wood products industries).
- Afforestation and reforestation.
- Carbon capture and storage.
- Energy efficiency improvements.

It is estimated that improved forest management for multiple purposes carried out on 50% of the existing plantations (including a 10% increase in plantation area between 2009 and 2030) will increase productivity of approximately 4,800 ha/yr. The total potential carbon sequestration capacity from improved forest management by 2030 is approximately 4,000,000 tCO2e.

Proposed project needs identified during the NAMA process will be addressed by the planned interventions. As sand for afforestation is a very scarce resource in Rwanda, the NAMA has prioritised forest restoration on degraded land. The proposed forestry programs include: (i) establishment of a national tree forest cover of 30% as envisioned in forestry policy papers, with particular attention to steep slopes in order to reduce soil erosion; and (ii) improved forest management through training for forest management personnel, inspection and information systems at MCE, RWFA, MINAGRI, and district extension services. The NAMA also includes the establishment of a national tree nursery, seed banks, and centers for new technology, equipment, and service. These are specific objectives of the project.

The tea and coffee sectors were selected for NAMA development under the industry section of the NAMA report. The tea sector is expected to lead to a GHG emission reduction potential of 146,000 tCO2e during the 2030 period. The coffee sector is expected to lead to a GHG emission reduction potential of 58,000 tCO2e and the economic potential for value addition in the downstream sections which involves building industrial capacity in the tea and coffee sectors.

The first NAMA applicable alternative scenario for energy efficiency improvements in the tea and coffee production is estimated to lead to a GHG emission reduction potential of 146,000 tCO2e during the 2030 period. The second NAMA applicable alternative scenario for energy efficiency improvements in the tea sector and the economic potential for value addition in the downstream sections which involves building industrial capacity in the tea and coffee sectors.

Adoption of energy measures in tea processing is estimated to result in a potential emission reduction of 146,000 tCO2e during the 2030 period. The proposed project includes provision for all six shortlisted scenarios: support for preparation of a NAMA design and documentation; pilot programs in the tea plants to demonstrate energy efficiency improvements; establishment of an energy efficiency improvement program rates to participating industries; establishing an insurance scheme to reduce risks for financial institutions in availing energy efficient technologies, bringing together a wider community of equipment suppliers, academic

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 65 OF 93

Institutions, owners and buyers to make energy efficiency affordable and available to all facilities. The proposed NAMA activities are expected to result in reduced consumption of carbon intense fuels (fuel based electricity generation, fuel based heat production) and the use of more efficient technologies. This will lead to a reduction in the demand for coal and other fossil fuels due to improved driving allowing the industry to seek for higher price at international auctions. Improvements in the use of energy efficient technologies will also reduce the cost of energy consumption.

The first NAMA applicable alternative emission scenario for the Housing: Building Sector is focused on household energy efficiency. The housing sector has a significant potential for GHG emissions reduction, as houses have the highest mitigation potential of 2 GtCO2eq per year, which can lead to millions of tons of CO2 avoided annually. This scenario focuses on the implementation of energy efficient technologies in buildings and leads to annual GHG emission reductions of up to 50 million tCO2 eq per year. The most notable co-benefits of the alternative emissions scenario are the reduction of air pollution, improved indoor air quality, and reduced household cost of energy.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a Climate Resilient Strategy (GCGR) is central to achieving the government key development target of becoming a developed climate-resilient, low-carbon economy by 2050. The planned interventions will contribute towards this goal through improved infrastructure; integration approach to land use and human settlements; and integrated water resources management. The proposed project will also help to deliver against two of the Big Wires: agro-forestry and forestry and five priority areas of the GCGR: climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction, sustainable livelihoods, planning and management as well as integrated water resource management and planning; sustainable forestry, agriculture and land use. The project will also contribute towards the achievement of the government’s climate change export on crops (and specifically tea and coffee) was also specifically highlighted as a priority in the alignment.

Planned Interventions will benefit from the existence of strong policy and institutional frameworks to address climate change issues. The introduction of comprehensive and coordinated policies, institutional arrangements, and strategies for addressing climate change has been a key focus area of government efforts over recent years. Rwanda has strong capacity to implement a GCF-funded project and consistently delivers results against targets. This is evidenced by the fact that Rwanda has received 100% of its approved funding from the Fund, due to the high level of good governance. The OECD rated Rwanda as one of the countries that uses aid effectively.

Rwanda has demonstrated comprehensive ownership over, and capacity to implement, a funded project. Rwanda has also established strong institutional frameworks and the accredited MIO has a reliable track record in implementing projects. This is evidenced by the fact that the Ministry has successfully demonstrated through its current management of USD 19 million from the Adaptation Fund to implement the Reduction of Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) project.

The project operates in 2 districts, Nyabihu and Musanze, covering approximately 400km² and extending across 6 administrative sectors. The project is being implemented by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Conservation (LANAC) project funded by GEF/World Bank), and the “Building resilience of communities living in vulnerable ecosystems through sustainable land management practices” project (funded by LDCF). MIO is the Ministry responsible for ensuring sustainable management and rational use of natural resources, including forests. The Ministry has a strong track record in the formulation of regulations and mobilising resources for the development of the sector. The Ministry is also

**responsibility for monitoring and evaluating the implementation of environment, climate change and natural resources management at the national level.**

As the national climate and environment fund, FONERIA was the original executing entity already managing the GEF funded projects in Nicaragua and is therefore well positioned to lead this project. The Environment and Climate Change Fund, FONERIA, was established by the GEF in 2012 as a trust fund for the implementation of the National Environment and Climate Change Strategy (NECCS) on which the design for the fund was based. Initial seed capital of GBP 23.5 million was provided to FONERIA in 2012 by the UK Department for International Development (DFID) as a contribution towards the GEF trust fund. Since then, DFID support and so far, the government has contributed USD 3.7 million demonstrating its commitment to supporting LDCF/SCCF projects in Nicaragua. FONERIA is now the primary vehicle through which additional environment and climate change finance is channelled into Nicaragua.

## E. Efficiency and Effectiveness

### E.E.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

#### Economic and, if appropriate, financial soundness of the project

Given the concessional status, the financial structure of the project is geared to grants. However, the high level of concessionality and the grant modality applied (delivered through direct access mechanisms) is in line with the GGCF’s policy on concessional finance. The project will be implemented by a public entity that primarily invests in public projects that are non-revenue generating or uses small grants for technical assistance activities.

The project design has been developed to ensure that the financial structure of the project reflects the fact that different barriers exist across the various intervention areas, and so GGCF finance needs to be tailored to address these. The project design also ensures that the GGCF is not exposed to any significant asymmetry and misaligned incentives) as well as policy failures and behavioural barriers in each case.

All interventions have been designed to maximise the use of GGCF grant finance and comply with the principles of good governance, including transparency and accountability. In all cases, the financial structure has been tailored to (i) the incremental cost or the risk premium required to make investments viable, i.e. to meet the additional costs associated with the GGCF’s concessional status; (ii) the need for a high level of monitoring (so far as is required to make activities financially viable, or to overcome risk perceptions) so for demonstration effect and/or to ensure that the GGCF’s investment is not misaligned with other public funds; (iii) the need for additional resources to be mobilised if an intervention needs to be scaled up to meet a technical assistance need, then further supported by TA to enable scale-up to co-finance technical assistance, or to meet the additional costs associated with the GGCF’s concessional status. The financial structure of all interventions activities for vulnerable groups in all cases, the interventions have been designed to address barriers and create the conditions necessary for a successful implementation. In all cases, the financial structure has been tailored to ensure there is no distortion or displacement of existing public or private sector funds.

For climate resilient agriculture, the project focuses on supporting adaptation to climate change impacts and the development of climate-resilient agricultural practices in the three priority sectors. The project will combine investment in awareness raising and market development to include non-timber forest products and other value-added activities.

of access to finance including capital. Alongside this, the project is providing information (climate risk information) to help address the larger scale investment needs in the industry, especially for the sowing of new tea plantations, providing technical assistance to encourage climate resilient planning and thus leveraging private sector finance.

For the sustainable forestry component, the project is implementing high quality demonstration plots, and providing training on best practices. The project has also provided financial incentives (in the form of subsidies) so as to transform the forestry sector in the district. However, it is recognised that the long paybacks on forestry projects make them unattractive for private investors. In addition, the current level of public finance (subsidies) requires additional incentives, and thus an additional financial instrument has been included with the introduction of a new type of loan product. This loan product provides a combination of financing (through public goods provision and provision of non-market benefits - ecosystem services).

The project is also providing technical assistance to help introduce basic energy efficiency measures, with financing from the finance for the environment fund. The project will provide training on best practices in energy efficiency and provide technical assistance to help introduce basic energy efficiency measures, with financing from the finance for the environment fund.

For the tea factory energy efficiency component, the grant finance is targeted at addressing information failures and risk perception around the use of financially viable (no and low-rent) energy efficient measures and technologies. The project will provide training on best practices in energy efficiency and provide technical assistance to help introduce basic energy efficiency measures, with financing from the finance for the environment fund.

The project has been designed to maximise the efficiency and effectiveness of the interventions above. For adaptation and mitigation components, the project has been assessed using two alternative methods. First, the analysis has assessed the cost (GCF) over one period (10 years), which is a simple assessment but does not fully capture cost-effectiveness. To address this, the assessment has quantified the marginal abatement cost of each intervention and demonstrated that the interventions are highly cost-effective in terms of lifetime of the investment (GCF) and co-finance: 10% discount rate (column 2). This marginal abatement cost analysis demonstrates that all interventions have a very low marginal abatement cost, which is less than 5 USD per tonne CO2 equivalent. The project also includes an energy efficiency component in the tea factory.

There is also investment in capacity building across the project at the district level, which will bring about large benefits in terms of greater effectiveness and efficiency of public interventions for mitigation and adaptation.

The cost-effectiveness analysis has demonstrated that all the proposed adaptation and mitigation components are extremely high, and a marginal abatement cost (present value: 10% DFR) has estimated the cost-effectiveness at -1.3.

For adaptation, the effectiveness and efficiency of the interventions is demonstrated through the economic assessment, set out in Section E8.3 and Section F1. This has assessed the overall net present value of each intervention over a period of 25 years (discount rate: 10%). The analysis has been conducted with sensitivity analysis). The latter provides a key metric showing the benefits that arise for every USD $ invested, which is the marginal abatement cost. For example, the BCR for climate risk adaptation and deforestation mitigation is 23.6; for forest resilience and tea offices, the BCR is 2.3-0.5; for forestry programme the BCR is -1.3 (excluding water supply).

For all mitigation and adaptation components, the interventions have been designed to be highly effective in terms of their ability to deliver on the desired outcomes, while also being cost-effective. The programme has been structured to deliver at scale by maximising the number of beneficiaries reached and GCF provided. The project will ensure that the interventions are delivered with a high level of efficiency appropriate to achieve the desired outputs, thereby delivering efficiency. The procurement approach - and the commercial and management arrangements - will also ensure the highest value for money for goods and

Even at a 13% discount rate, the project still has a high overall net present value positive effect, although the lower discount rate affects longer-lived investments, notably reducing the BCR of forestry to 1.

### A financial analysis is conducted for each investment component, including those that involve market sectors. The financial analysis assesses the return on investment and adopts a different perspective to the CBA, which focuses on costs and benefits in terms of economic value (e.g., revenue, cost savings, etc.) and includes taxes and charges (which are excluded in the economic appraisal). It is undertaken from the perspective of the project sponsor and considers the financial viability of each component. The analysis takes into account cash flows and assesses the feasibility of a project or investment. The BCR is the ratio that the NPV is zero, and it is computed as the ratio between the present value of benefits and costs.

The analysis shows that the financial rate of return for project investments (GCF finance and co-finance) is high in all sub-components except energy (biomass). This is due to the combination of mitigation and adaptation components. For the climate resilient production of tea and coffee, the BCR is 2.0, which means that every dollar invested will yield a return of $2.0 over time.

### The financial analysis also shows that the project investments are financially viable for all sub-components as a whole, due to the combination of mitigation and adaptation components. For the climate resilient production of tea and coffee, the BCR is 2.0, which means that every dollar invested will yield a return of $2.0 over time.

### The high underlying IRR in the project investments demonstrates their financial viability in the long-term. The national mainstreaming activities in response to disinvestment and awareness will ensure their continuation. # RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 71 OF 93

The innovations and cost-effective solutions to reduce deforestation and water losses have helped to reduce energy consumption.

The innovations and market enabling conditions that have facilitated penetration of these technologies particularly in rural communities include the introduction of the telephone scratch card usage, loan schemes for purchase of solar home systems, and tax incentives. In some cases, financial institutions have been introduced in accelerating home solar water heaters. In some instances, tax incentives have been applied to promote the use of solar water heaters.

### Watersheds:

While Rekords is committed to restore 2,000,000 ha of degraded lands and forests by 2030, the national government has developed a framework for watershed management. The framework includes land management including small scale water harvesting for household use146, agriculture and agroforestry on existing farms, and afforestation and reforestation in degraded areas. Rekords is committed to using these techniques building on these best practices. A number of approaches are working with national and district authorities to develop watershed management plans that will be implemented through the use of appropriate techniques adapted to each landscape. The packages includes tailored agroforestry technologies as well as water management techniques such as small scale water harvesting, terracing, contour ploughing and other land management practices.

In addition to this RWFA147 has over a long period had developed agroforestry tools and models used by farmer field schools in the area. The department of agriculture and natural resources at the national level and the department at the ministry of natural resources also developed a series of tools in water resource management that will be integrated into the watershed management plans.

The project will work with the local communities to ensure that the best practices are adopted. Nyungwe catchments both covering Gumbu districts have established watershed management committees and there is a strong commitment from the community to protect these resources.

### Tea and coffee:

The investment has focused on energy efficiency best practice technology for tea factories, as these are the major source of emissions in the tea industry. The design study phase, which undertook detailed energy audits at the factory level, identified that the largest energy savings can be achieved by introducing technologies in Rwanda that can reduce energy use, generate cost savings and reduce emission. These will be used to demonstrate best practice technology for tea factories. The same approach is being adopted with respect to coffee factories as the major source of emissions.

The current project will also focus on the introduction of new tea varieties and coffee species on efficiency energy saving technologies for new tea farms (a further ten major plantations are proposed, as part of the national tea strategy). It is expected that the introduction of these new tea varieties and coffee species in addition to the effect of taking both current and new industrial plants in the project will have a transformational impact on the sector. The project will also focus on the introduction of climate resilient tea and coffee production.

The component is undertaking climate resilient pilots for tea and coffee production, demonstrating the use of climate smart agricultural practices that are appropriate for Rwanda’s agro-ecological zones and the expansion of these cash crops in Rwanda. The information on climate resilient agriculture will be integrated into the existing farmer Field School programmes for these crops, ensuring best practice is disseminated as scale at national level.

### Forestry:

The entire intervention is premised on application of best practice. RWFA already holds excellent best practices in terms of forest management and tree planting. These are based on the experience gained from the Bodi project (the Tree Plantation Establishment and Management and Tree Harvesting Techniques manuals amongst others). The project will build on these best practices by scaling up their implementation through their application, developing national capacity in their best practice approaches, methods and techniques for forest management and tree planting. These best practices are being applied across the project area. The techniques described are based on the accumulated experience of plantation forestry in Rwanda. They include the establishment of a nursery, selection of species, site preparation, selective plantation forestry to be undertaken on the challenging sites normally available for tree planting. These best practices will be scaled up and disseminated through farmer field schools and other extension services.

The project will also focus on the development of guidelines and the guidelines with the Sawley Promotion Grant Scheme in Uganda and if implemented, a similar scheme could be developed in Rwanda to promote the establishment of tea and coffee plantations in Gumbu.

### Climate resilient settlements:

The project will adopt and improve on the successful IOD model village concept (a national policy) to house socially disadvantaged social groups living in high risk zones from flooding and landslides. The project will focus on the development of a climate resilient settlement based on best practice, housing houses into the hillsides in that way that reduces the use of cut out fill soil as well as building construction materials. This will be demonstrated at the Gumbu pilot site and scaled up to other sites across Rwanda.

### Economic and Financial Analysis

#### F.1. Economic and Financial Analysis

Provide the rationale and details for the detailed economic and financial analysis including the financial model, taking into consideration the following:

The results of the economic and financial analysis were set out in Section E.6.3. This presented the economic appraisal, which demonstrates that the project has a high net present value (NPV), a high internal rate of return (IRR) and a high benefit to cost ratio, demonstrating that this project has a very high net present value (at 12% discount rate) and is financially viable.

The watershed protection involves a suite of interventions. This intervention addresses the current high economic costs associated with soil erosion, sedimentation in water bodies, reduced agricultural productivity, loss of biodiversity, landslides, and floods, as well as the effects of rainfall variability. The intervention is primarily focused on increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change by improving soil fertility, reducing erosion, and water conservation, and rain water harvesting.

The GCF investment will primarily be in public goods and ecosystem services, which are not directly marketed or sold but provide benefits for society as a whole. These include direct financial benefits for some options (notably agroforestry) but the main benefits are economic (non-market), and also indirect benefits such as improved soil fertility, reduced erosion, increased water retention, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions. The economic analysis shows a high benefit to cost ratio from these interventions, extending beyond the project period.

Investments in climate resilient production of tea and coffee, target two of the top exports of Rwanda (valuing USD 105 million and USD 238 million respectively). These are both important export sectors, on both terms of productivity and quality, which is reducing small holder incomes as well as affecting export revenues/incomes. The economic benefits are high (including environmental benefits), and they also show a high benefit to cost ratio from these interventions.

The GCF finance is being used to provide technical assistance in the form of farmer field schools to support climate resilient agricultural practices. These interventions will increase the resilience of smallholder farmers, whose livelihoods are highly vulnerable to climate change. The interventions also target new Government agricultural development plans to increase the land area of production under climate-resilient agriculture and agroforestry systems. This is important because many existing agricultural interventions that will become unsuitable in the future (10). The project will invest in mainstreaming information (public good) to help Smallholder farmers adapt their farming practices, which will reduce impacts on soil fertility, increase yields, and reduced impacts of climate change, and thus the protection of future production, revenues and exports. Finally,

beem targeted to provide technical assistance, provide support for public goods, and create the enabling environment to address barriers, leveraging on public - and where possible – private finance.

### F.2 Technical Exclusion

Description of exclusion criteria for technical exclusion and if a particular technology has been chosen, describe why it is the best option.

A range of innovative technologies and best practices will be promoted under the project. These technologies will be applied based on an understanding of the specific local context of the settlement, land or infrastructure use, and the availability of resources at each site. The following technologies will be included:

- **Soil conservation:**
  - Contour ploughing
  - Agroforestry drill pits
  - Straw strip pits
  - Tree planting in forests

- **Sustainable forest management:** 
  - Improved seedlings, planting techniques and spacing of trees to site conditions.
  - Improved agroforestry systems (e.g. intercropping)
  - Improved livestock grazing practices
  - Afforestation
  - Low carbon building materials: improved brick-making technologies and solid compressed earth blocks; appropriate technology for the local context

The application of best practices and technologies is discussed earlier in this proposal in Section E.4.6 The technologies and approaches that are proposed in this proposal have each undergone a rigorous options analysis process, as described in Annex 2.1.3. A summary description of the project’s approach to selection and a detailed description of various technologies and their appropriateness are provided in the three component reports (attached as reports 2, 3 & 4 respectively) that have been prepared with support from the PPF.

### F.3 Environmental Social Assessment: Including Gender Considerations

The ESDA will be conducted at each site to assess the environmental and social dimensions of the proposed project and how these are likely to change over time as a result of the project implementation, including the potential for adverse impacts on the environment and human health. The ESDA and gender analysis are attached as separate reports under Annex 1.

Environmental Social Assessment (ESA) assessments have been conducted for this project as a whole and to assess each intervention and mitigating measure proposed in the proposal based on the principles of the ESDA and the gender analysis.

The project is expected to have large positive impacts on the environment as much of the GCF investment will be directed towards ecosystem-based adaptation and interventions that reduce emissions. The main potential adverse environmental impacts of the project is the potential modification of hydrological flows for development of water harvesting structures, which may result in increased sedimentation in the catchment area of the tea resilience measures to be implemented in Mulindu tea plantations. The agricultural feasibility study identified the need for a man-made reservoir to provide irrigation water and control flooding during the rainy season. Increasing occurrence of flooding in recent years due to erratic and intense rainfall events. Unusually low, the precipitation levels experienced over the last two seasons have resulted in reduced soil moisture content, increasing issue of rain variability and extreme dry years with increasing dry spell duration. Irrigation is considered as a key intervention for improving agricultural productivity and reducing the risk of crop failure and avoid production losses and livelihood deterioration.

The man-made reservoir will be constructed using irrigation scheme to alleviate flooding and to improve the availability of water. Controlling water flows in the manmade will mitigate flooding caused by over base flow of rivers and seasonal rainfall runoff. The reservoir will also provide a supply of water for tea fields in the dry season. Construction works for the manland surface drainage system. Primary and secondary surface drains are also planned which will work as a secondary catchment area to control floodwater. These measures have the potential to destabilise the receiving downstream users and temporary destabilise the ecosystem dependent on the current river flow. During the dry season, water will be drawn from the stream thereby reducing

## How quantities, changing flood flows and affecting biodiversity downstream. The ESIA has recommended that the original design shall:

- avoid modification of hydrological flows for downstream users of Mulindi river stream;
- incorporate release of water from the central drain to maintain a specific water level downstream that can be used by the community for irrigation purposes (e.g., 100 mm);
- consider measures to avoid destructive flooding effects, such as the possibility of occurrence of floods in ≥20 or >100 year return period;
- consider measures to reduce sedimentation and erosion;
- consider measures for minor water logging; salinization; siltation; or scouring of drains or canals.

## During implementation, the ESIA recommends that:

- ensure that the design of the irrigation distribution maintenance of the irrigation infrastructure and realize using convex over water distribution within the main channel;
- use a green buffer zone (GBZ) with Bananas species to establish at least 10 m from the central drain and primary side canals of the project area, as well as along the banks of the main canal and secondary canals. The GBZ will help in reducing soil erosion and sedimentation within the irrigation canals;
- ensure that the design of the irrigation distribution maintenance of the irrigation infrastructure and realize using convex over water distribution within the main channel;

The GBZ will help in reducing soil erosion and sedimentation within the irrigation canals;
- ensure that the design of the irrigation distribution maintenance of the irrigation infrastructure and realize using convex over water distribution within the main channel.

These recommendations have been included in the final design, workshop and budget.

## Social assessment:

The ESIA has identified a number of potential social risks and impacts that are all covered in the published Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) for the agricultural land for the Kangla settlement. A comprehensive Abreviated Resettlement action plan (RAP) or Inland resettlement Action Plan (IAP) will be prepared to address these risks and impacts.

Moreover, compensation at full replacement cost with reference to Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) will be provided to all affected agricultural land owners for the loss of their livelihoods due to project implementation. In addition, to avoid income losses from missed cultivation due to delays in compensation and construction of the settlement.

Affected agricultural land owners shall be allowed to cultivate their land until only seasonal crops as they have been doing so far. The period during which they will not be able to cultivate their land is 120 days after they have received compensation; and

For livestock, the affected farmers will be provided with a subsidy of Kshs. 500 per day for 120 days after they have received compensation; and

For fish farming, the affected farmers will be provided with a subsidy of Kshs. 300 per day for 120 days after they have received compensation.

Other social risks include: local resistance against proposed watershed protection intervention on their private land; occupational health hazards during construction and operation of settlements and production facilities; and the potential for adverse working conditions especially child labour and exclusion of women from employment for certain jobs and training.

## Gender analysis:

The gender analysis identified a number of gender barriers and inequalities in Rwanda and Gicumbi District. The main gender barriers are: lack of awareness on the importance of gender equality, which is gender-inclusive and -responsive during implementation; Understanding and championing—as opposed to giving lip service—to gender equality; and women's limited access to resources (land, water, finance). Critical barriers that the project will tackle is that typically men have greater access to, and control over monetary benefits from the project. Women are more likely to be involved in domestic work (e.g., cooking, cleaning, making, tea-plucking etc.) while women's access to these activities are limited and hinder their ability to generate sufficient income for themselves and their families. The project will address this by ensuring that women are costed measures to ensure the equitable participation of women and men in the project; these have been listed below:

- Interventions that prioritize accessible and equal training opportunities for women and men and ensure that they participate equally—fully and effectively, as well as address the differential knowledge and experiences of women and men;
- Equal access to resources (land, water, finance) for women and men. This is because it has been found that gender parity is a requirement) and enforcement of the relevant provisions of the 2009 labour law; ensuring that women are included in all aspects of project planning, implementation and monitoring and evaluation;
- Kangra recognizing that these households are particularly vulnerable to climate change.

The project will also ensure that women's participation in decision-making processes is increased. This includes providing training on gender equality for women and men in the community, particular biogas and water harvesting which significantly reduce the burden on women and children having to walk long distances for water and firewood).

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND BUDGETARY FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 78 OF 93

On the under Law No. 4/2013 of 25/05/2013 FONERWA operated under the MoC (the accredited entity) with the Permanent Secretary of MoC as the Chief Budget Manager (CBM). Under the new Law revised in 20/07/16, FONERWA is an autonomous institution will be assessed for financial management capacity. This will be done by the MoF.

Project funds from the GCF will be received into a dedicated MDE bank account held at the National Bank of Tanzania (NBT) and managed under the supervision of FONERWA. The NBT will provide some project activities. Accounting for the advance of grants will be managed through FMIS. Additional reports from the MoF will be required to monitor the progress of projects. Financial balances on the basis of each grant and advances balances will be obtained by FONERWA.

The selection of partner institutions will be managed by FONERWA's Fund Management Committee. The pre-identification of partners will be done in consultation with the GCF. Partners will be required to have management systems to manage, properly disburse funds and account for grant funds. Legally binding grants will be issued to partners on a "no recourse" basis. This means that the partner is not responsible for any "open" basis. All implementing partners will be required to provide quarterly financial reports on grant status as well as monthly progress reports on project activities. The MoF will also require annual financial statements from each partner institution.

The capacity of FONERWA's Fund Management Committee and the FMIS system for the project will be strengthened by the appointment of a number of key posts for the duration of the project:

1. An accountant based in the PMO to manage financial accounting and reporting arrangements;
2. A technical advisor to provide advice on the management systems, operations, and training of partner systems and all other related matters;
3. A procurement specialist; and
4. A legal advisor.

These partners will work with their institutional counterparts and will ensure that fiduciary risk is identified and managed. All supporting financial records will be properly filed and retained to facilitate internal control as well as external audit requirements. The GCF will have access to all relevant documents at any time.

The location, condition and cost of assets will be periodically checked on a sample basis. Financial reporting will be done in accordance with the GCF's financial reporting standards (i.e., quarterly financial reports and bank reconciliations) are monthly. Consolidated financial reports will be presented to the Project Steering Committee for approval at each meeting.

Financial reports will be provided to the GCF in accordance with Clause 17 of the AMA which states:

(a) on a semi-annual basis within sixty (60) days after 30 June or 31 December of each year (or such other time as may be agreed by the parties);

(b) the dates and amounts disbursed for Funded Activities, for the period reported and cumulative amounts up to the period, broken down by each Funded Activity, and compliance with Clause 17(2)(a), 17.03(a) and 17.04(c) of this Agreement;

(iii) the actual expenditures for the Funded Activities for the period reported and cumulative amounts up to the period, broken down by each Funded Activity; and

(iv) (A) the date on which any Funded Activity is officially closed; (B) the final amount disbursed for such Funded Activity; (C) the amount of any unused funds from such Funded Activity that are returned to the GCF Account; and (D) the actual expenditures for the period reported and cumulative amounts up to the period, broken down by each Funded Activity;

(v) the amount of any unused Funds received by the Accredited Entity from Funded Activities, as well as the amount of such Unused Funds paid to the Fund for the period reported and cumulative amounts up to the period, broken down by each Funded Activity; and

(vi) a statement of Investment Income earned on GCF Proceeds, as well as the amount of such investment income.

(b) within two (2) months after the end of the GCF Fiscal Year, an unaudited final financial statement for each Funded Activity in accordance with Clause 17(3)(a), 17.05(a) and 17.06(c); and with Clauses 17(2)(c), 17.03(b) and 17.04(d) of this Agreement (with specific Funded Activities listed in a separate annexure). The final financial statement will be accompanied by an unaudited final financial statement for each of the Funded Activities and the GCF Account containing the same information;

(c) within two (2) months after expiration or termination of this Agreement, an unaudited final financial

### Internal Audit

An internal auditor is provided for as part of MCF’s overall oversight role. This will be a full time post funded through the ACF fee. This person will perform MCF’s & FONERWA’s internal audit capacity, with a mandate to assess the integrity and effectiveness of the financial management systems used by GCF Funds.

The Internal Auditor will also be subject to internal audit, which will be implemented through a compliance based annual Internal Audit (IA) workplan. The IA workplan will be developed in consultation with MCF’s Project Management Unit and will cover all financial management systems used to record, manage and report on GCF Funds. The following activities will be carried out:

- **Appoint an Internal Audit Committee** according to the internal Audit Charter included at Annex 18
- An Internal Auditor will be recruited for the duration of the project, in accordance with the terms of reference provided by MCF. The IA workplan will be developed and approved by the IA Committee. Any audit findings to the Internal Audit Committee not related to the Project Management Unit will provide the necessary recommendations.
- **The Internal Auditor’s scope of work** will include the following:
  - Assessment of the financial management systems of all entities receiving GCF funds either directly or indirectly
  - All procurements carried out under the project, by MCF, FONERWA or other entities receiving GCF funds

In carrying out this work the Internal Auditor will:

- Prepare an annual internal audit workplan which will be updated on a rolling quarterly basis and which will cover all financial management systems. The IA workplan will be reviewed to ensure that internal controls (as designed and implemented) are likely to prevent or detect errors and whether there is a reasonable assurance of achieving the objectives for value for money.
- Ensure that the annual workplan responds to priority areas at risk
- Where particular areas of concern or increasing risk are found, amend the workplan to respond accordingly
- Present results to the Internal Audit Committee together with recommendations to improve financial management

Public service entities are required to maintain internal audit functions. All budget entities must comply with the Organic Law and the associated financial regulations providing the legal framework for the management and accounting of public funds. The Public Service Entities will be subject to an annual audit by the National Auditors’ Office in accordance with the National Auditing Standards and Procedures for Financial Management and Accounting.

The Internal Auditor appointed by the project will be allowed access to internal audit workshops and internal and external audits conducted by the National Auditors’ Office, as well as other relevant entities.

- An annual internal audit report prepared by the Internal FONERWA on project implementation processes for submission to MCF
- Bi-annual internal audit report prepared by the Internal Audit Committee on project implementation processes for submission to the PSC

A comprehensive list of project entities prepared by FONERWA, and also including audited financial statements for the activities directly implemented by FONERWA for submission to MCF; audited annual financial statements for the project prepared by an MCF External Auditor for submission to:

**To ensure that their duty is discharged all Memoranda of Understanding and contracts between the executing and other entities are subject to internal audit. The Internal Audit Committee will be responsible for ensuring that all projects meet the marked purposes and whether procurements are complying with the principles set out in Paragraph 3.1 of this manual.**

### External Audits
MINIRENA and the District of Gicumbi are audited annually by the Office of the Auditor General for State Procurement. The Ministry of Finance, Revenue and Public Service (MIFERPS) – which already receives funds from other development partners (KfW and DFID) – is additionally subject to periodic thematic audits by the Office of the Auditor General. The GCF will also be audited annually by the Public Accounts Committee. Provision can be made for additional audit to be commissioned by the GCF and conducted independently on specific systems and procedures. In line with the AAM, the project will be audited annually by independent external auditors.

The project has defined a transparent grievance mechanism which allows any direct or indirect beneficiary to submit complaints regarding the implementation of the project. The complainant can lodge their complaint in writing directly at the Office of the Auditor General or at the Office of the Ombudsman if the complaint is not satisfied by the response at lower levels. Officers working on the project are required to report any irregularities to the GCF and/or the Office of the Auditor General. The project establishes a Code of Professional Ethics for Public Servants. In addition senior management staff are required to disclose their assets in accordance with the law. The project will also establish an annual basis to establish the rate of asset accumulation is commensurate with expected income levels.

### Procurement
Procurements will be conducted under the regulatory regime in Rwanda which applies directly to all Government procurement and includes the Law No 12/2007 of 27/03/2007 or public procurement as amended to date.
- Law N° 12/2007 of 27/03/2007 on public procurement as amended to date.

These regulations embed the principles of fairness, transparency, accountability and the promotion of competition in all public procurements. The project will ensure that all procured goods and services under grant funds the project is implementing a pre-award due diligence process to ensure partner organisations have in place adequate systems for managing their procurement processes in accordance with applicable Rwandan public sector regulations. FONERWA’s procurement function will be supplemented by a small procurement team, which will be responsible for ensuring that all procurements are conducted in line with the building sessions that are regularly provided to staff within public institutions.

More details on the project’s financial management system including financial accounting,
disbursement methods and auditing are provided in the Project Implementation Manual (Annex 5).

### G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

#### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Description:** Low quality of farmers' awareness about the proposed watershed protection measures.
**Risk category:** High
**Level of impact:** Moderate
**Probability of risk occurrence:** High

The Community Adaptation Facility is intended to enhance the uptake of the proposed watershed protection measures. The Facility will be established through the project and managed (with assistance) by communities themselves, to: provide training on watershed protection; establish a community-based organization; mobilize community ownership in project activities; and ensure that the project also uses demonstration plots and learning exchanges to raise awareness of the benefits of watershed protection.

#### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Description:** 5 years is insufficient time for farmers' adoption.
**Risk category:** Moderate
**Level of impact:** Low
**Probability of risk occurrence:** Moderate

The focus of the projects is on low-yield options, and these will provide immediate benefits in relation to productivity. This will allow smallholder farmers to see a return on their investment within 5 years. However, this is a particularly high-risk scenario.

#### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Description:** High costs of improved conservation practices.
**Risk category:** Financial
**Level of impact:** High
**Probability of risk occurrence:** Moderate

The investment in the promotional activities will demonstrate the savings that can be achieved with the new technology. The investment is also intended to ensure that the project has a strong financial base and targets appropriate groups.

#### Selected Risk Factor 4
**Description:** Operational grants for the CAF are not provided by FONERIA.
**Risk category:** High
**Level of impact:** Moderate

Community grants for the CAF will be used for the grants. GCF finance will be used to support the TA to set up and operate the fund.

### Measuring Measures

The project planning will be aligned with GCF systems and processes and will be fully communicated to all stakeholders. Districts will be fully engaged in the planning of activities. Activity reporting is required in District Executive Management Meeting. The project will seek to minimize the probability of project failure and maximize the probability of project achievement. This risk mitigation strategy will also reduce the probability of the risk occurring to units.

### Other Potential Risks to the Project

The project will also monitor emerging risks that may arise during the lifetime of the project. These include:

1. **Changes in climate conditions**: The project will monitor changes in climate conditions and their impact on the adaptation activities, including the potential for increased frequency or intensity of extreme weather events.
2. **Changes in policy environment**: The project will monitor changes in national policies that may affect the project's implementation and its ability to achieve its objectives.

### H.1. Logic Framework

#### H.1.1. Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund Level

The project proposes addressing existing climate risks, but also implements early actions that start building for future climate change impacts. The project will support the development of a resilient urban environment through improved infrastructure construction. GCF funds reserve critical watershed functions that support the majority of country’s water resources. The project will also provide technical assistance to build capacity in the use of natural resources and land-based resources using planning, which will facilitate a shift away from subsistence farming towards more sustainable livelihoods.

An explicit objective of the project is to inform and engage the public. This has a high potential to cost effectively increase climate resilience by building social capital through increased awareness and knowledge about climate change. The project will also support the development of an urban water management plan, which will be developed in collaboration with the Water Foundation. The results of the interventions are seen as the knowledge and experience developed during implementation.

The project has a high potential to contribute to sustainable development by increasing the resilience of communities to climate change impacts through improved infrastructure construction. The project also has a high potential to contribute to sustainable development by increasing the resilience of communities to climate change impacts through improved infrastructure construction.

### 1. Watershed protection and climate-resilient agriculture

#### 1.1 Selecting of areas enriched with biodiversity and ecosystem services

- 1.1.2 Awareness raising of climate change impacts
- 1.1.3 Training or project staff and community members on climate change adaptation planning
- 1.1.4 Community based survey of project area to identify the most vulnerable communities, water and agricultural experts
- 1.1.5 Community meetings and planning sessions with stakeholders to develop a plan with options for land use, agriculture, pasture, and other uses.
- 1.1.6 District Land Use Plan as sub-commitment under the National Land Use Plan
- 1.1.7 Development of Community Adaptation Plans

#### 1.2 Reduce slope erosion to sustainable levels

- 1.2.1 Conservation with Renewable Agricultural Practices (CWRAP) and other soil conservation practices on land set aside for agriculture, with emphasis on contour plowing, etc., with the use of mulches, cover crops, and grasses.
- 1.2.2 Awareness campaign on erosion control measures and their benefits
- 1.2.3 Introduction of erosion control measures on existing terraces and construction of biological control measures and afforestation in areas where soil is eroding.
- 1.2.4 Capacity building of local authorities to monitor the effectiveness of erosion control measures, including monitoring and reporting.
- 1.2.5 Matching crop and permanent vegetation with land use and farming methods to the terrain characteristics, such as contour plowing and sub-plotting, setting up terraces, and brush layering.

#### 1.3 Slashline rows, mulches and other slopes with protective forestry

- Support to establish protective forests on land set aside for agriculture, with emphasis on contour plowing, etc., with the use of mulches, cover crops, and grasses.
- Introduction and erosion control measures on existing terraces and construction of biological control measures and afforestation in areas where soil is eroding.

#### 1.4 Integrate agro-forestry into land management (up to 50% for the watershed)

- Support to increase the use of agro-forestry practices, such as contour hedgerows, terraces, and other slope protection measures.
- Common hedgerows, terraces, and in Foster Areas, the establishment of windbreaks, shelterbelts, and erosion and water control structures (e.g., check dams).
- Mulches and erosion and water and soil conservation practices such as contour plowing, etc.

#### 1.5 Support 1800 smallholder farmers to adopt agro-ecological approaches to increase their livelihood production

- Support to establish protective forests on land set aside for agriculture, with emphasis on contour plowing, etc., with the use of mulches, cover crops, and grasses.
- Introduction and erosion control measures on existing terraces and construction of biological control measures and afforestation in areas where soil is eroding.

#### 1.6 Support 300 smallholder farmers to adopt agro-ecological approaches to increase their livelihood production

- Planting trees/seedlings on existing terraces (up to 50% for the watershed) with emphasis on contour plowing, etc., with the use of mulches, cover crops, and grasses.
- Planting trees/seedlings within crop areas (must not exceed 10%) in the following areas: 400 m² per household in home gardens; 2500 m² per household in agroforestry plots; 9000 m² per household in pasture and other uses.
- Planting trees/seedlings within crop areas (must not exceed 10%) in the following areas: 400 m² per household in home gardens; 2500 m² per household in agroforestry plots; 9000 m² per household in pasture and other uses.

### 3. Climate resilient ecosystems

- Implement agroforestry systems that store carbon in trees and soil.
- Promote sustainable land management practices such as crop rotation, cover cropping, and conservation tillage.

#### 4.2 Awareness Building: Progress towards awareness to support motivation and scale up in other districts

- **Awareness raising:** Increasing understanding of the project's goals, the importance of implementing the project to support the achievement of these goals, and the need for local target district authorities, and other stakeholders (e.g., farmers) to participate fully in the project (as lessons learned).
  - This will include building awareness of the project’s goals, importance, and benefits.

#### 4.3 Increase Capacity of Local Institutions for Sustainable Forest Management, Soil Protection and Climate Resilient Settlements

- **Local institutions:** Will include capacity support for sustainable forest management (e.g., tree planting), soil protection and sustainable design and maintenance of climate resilient settlements.

### 4.4 Mainstream approaches to climate resilient approaches into existing forestry programs and projects to support scale up and replication

This sub-component will support REDD+ to integrate good forest management practices into the national REDD+ strategy, including: 4.4.1 Developing a framework for forest cover area revision and management interventions 4.4.2 Mainstreaming support for integration of climate resilient approaches in forestry development and investments in the country 4.4.3 Mobilizing funding for scale-up and replication 4.4.4 Assessing and evaluating the effectiveness of climate resilient approaches

### 4.5 Mainstream approaches to climate resilient agriculture for smallholder farmers to support scale up and replication

The project will mainstream the project into policies and plans at the national level to ensure that climate change is considered in agricultural development programs. The agro-ecological approaches will be integrated with existing extension services, and improved extension materials along with training of extension workers on best practices.

### 4.6 Mainstream climate resilient approaches for agriculture extension programs to support scale up and replication

The project will mainstream the project into the National Agriculture Extension Program (NAEP) for testing, allowing the curriculum to be revised in line with the project’s approach. The NAEP will target at least 10 sites as co-operative demonstration sites.

### 4.7 Mainstream energy efficiency into the industry to support scale up and replication

The project will provide the uptake of energy efficient technologies in industries, varying from small-scale enterprises to large factories. The project will mainstream in current and future sector strategies and plans, including the development of a national energy strategy and action plan. The project will hold a workshop on energy efficiency standards for new factories.

## H-2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

### Implementation Arrangements for Monitoring and Evaluation

The project will use the existing monitoring framework and M&E procedures that have been used for other development funds such as SFD, KWF and UNDP. Detailed workplans are developed under each of these components to ensure alignment with the overall project outcome themes and connected to higher level impact statement and indicators. Clear and coherent output, results and impact indicators will be defined in a logical sequence and linked to outputs, together with time-bound milestones and targets.

The M&E framework will use a system of progress against both process and performance objectively verifiable indicators (OVIs) and will be used to identify areas for specific management attention and will result in revision to annual implementation plans and, where necessary in year plans and budgets. Implementing partners


---
**Page 93**

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDING FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 92 OF 93

will be responsible for reporting against the agreed indicators, under the oversight of the dedicated PMU supported by the FONERWA team. Future disbursements will be considered in the light of the assessment of performance and impact made at the end of each year.

The MEE will monitor and evaluate the project through a series of reviews and appraisals of evidence throughout the project lifetime to optimise performance and impact.

FONERWA currently has two MEE Specialists and two additional posts are proposed:

- 1. A MEE Specialist to support the implementation of the project, including detailed operational guidelines.
- 2. A MEE officer, based in the District Office at the point of service provision, to undertake data collection and field visits.

The MEE Specialists will conduct six monthly visit/spot-checks; with a particular focus on 'at risk' projects.

### Progress Monitoring

Quarterly physical and financial progress reports against workplan and budget will be developed to ensure compliance with project milestones and the achievement of outputs within the agreed time frame. These reports will provide an update on progress in the delivery of work stream activities outturns contributing to the achievement of project outputs. The MEE Specialists will also conduct spot checks to monitor work plan updates and budgets for the next quarter. The reports shall also describe progress on implementation as well as any challenges encountered, including lessons learned, and the level of sustainability and acceptance of project interventions by the stakeholders particularly the beneficiaries.

At the end of each year an Annual Progress Review (APR) or lesson learning exercise to assess provisional impact will be conducted. This will include a review of the work plan, budget and progress against milestones with implementers with support from the MEE Specialists as needed and may be outsourced for some activities (eg an annual assessment of project performance). The APR will also include a review of lessons learned by a stakeholder workshop/stakeholder community visit and case studies and will report on progress made against the indicators. The APR will be submitted to the Secretariat within 30 days after the end of each year via the AMA. The APR will be submitted to the Secretariat on an annual basis for the period ending on 31 December within sixty (60) days from the end of such period. The MEE Specialists will also conduct a mid-term review every quarter reporting quarterly. The PMU will prepare an annual work plan which will include proposed activities, detailed plans, and budget estimates to be submitted to the Secretariat for approval by the Monitoring and Evaluation committee for review and approval. The project timeline will provide a complete set of indicators for monitoring progress against the approved workplan.

Project evaluations will assess the effectiveness, efficiency and sustainability of interventions and partner institutions' performance in implementing the project.

- data compiled in the management information systems of implementing partners;
- mid-term and final evaluations that use an array of indicators to assess the effectiveness, efficiency, relevance, sustainability and impact of the project.

### Baseline and Independent Evaluations

In the first year of implementation a baseline assessment will be conducted. An independent midterm evaluation will be conducted at the end of Year 3 to assess project performance. It will also determine whether or not the strategy adopted for achieving the project's outputs and outcomes has been effective, and what lessons were made relevant. It will critically review progress towards the paradigm shift objectives and suggest areas for development. The final evaluation will be conducted in the last year of implementation (Year 5) to assess management. It will present the initial lessons of project design, implementation and management and make recommendations on how these lessons can be applied to future projects. The evaluation will also provide a critical review of the project's performance against its objectives and outputs.

The findings will be used to enhance implementation during the final half of the project’s term and to learn lessons for future projects. The evaluation will assess the extent to which the project has achieved its intended impact, sustainability, replicability and scaling-up. All monitoring and evaluation activities will align with Monitoring and Accountability Framework.

### Methodologies for monitoring and reporting of the key outcomes of the project

The MEE system will track and assess the extent to which supported interventions result in changes that are relevant, sustainable and equitable. The MEE will also monitor and evaluate the sustainability of outputs as well as consideration of unintended negative effects. Monitoring will include internal qualitative and quantitative data collection and analysis. The MEE will use a combination of methods for monitoring and evaluation such as desk review, field visits, interviews, focus group discussions, surveys and other appropriate approaches to ensure balance between breadth and depth, and bias in selecting only success stories. Monitoring will be

Based on the annual work plans, the logic framework, theory of change and results-based indicators. The result measurement will also include a focus on value for money (across economy, efficiency, and effectiveness) indicators in addition to impact of supported interventions.

### Lesson Learning and Replication
The results from the M&E system will inform the replication and scaling up of project interventions. Data and quantitative information collected through the M&E system will be used to generate knowledge and learning products linked to organizing, reflecting and using information gained. The knowledge management system will generate lessons, best practices, success stories, and other feedback coming from stakeholders and partners. Success stories and other feedback will inform the replication and scaling up of project interventions. Data and quantitative information collected through the M&E system will be used to generate knowledge and learning products linked to organizing, reflecting and using information gained. The qualitative data will be combined with qualitative assessment and beneficiary feedback from the knowledge management system to generate lessons, best practices, success stories, and other feedback coming from stakeholders and project partners.

Kigali, Republic of Rwanda

Dear Sir,

RE: Funding proposal for the GCF by Ministry of Environment regarding Strengthening Climate Resilience of Rural Communities in Northern Rwanda (SCRNFR)

We refer to the project - Strengthening Climate Resilience of Rural Communities in Northern Rwanda (SCRNFR) - as outlined in the funding proposal submitted by the Ministry of Environment to us on 25th February 2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Rwanda Environment Management Authority, the National Designated Authority of Rwanda.

Pursuant to GCF decision 9/OW/36, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed and hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of Rwanda has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Rwanda's national priorities, strategies and plans.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all components and/or activities to be implemented within the scope of the project.
